# MLQT Backlog

The working list. Every open item has an id, and **an id is never reused** — they are cited from
code comments, test summaries, build scripts and CI workflows, so a new item takes the next number
above the highest ever issued, whatever has since been closed.

**B1–B408 have been issued.** B1–B167 were opened between 2026-09-03 and 2026-09-17 by the
seventeen end-of-branch reviews of the CI/CD toolchain and by phases 7a and 7b; B168–B203 by the
first end-to-end pass over the Photino release on 2026-09-17; B204 while settling B198's layout
question, B205 while fixing B192, B206 while trying to reproduce B198, B207–B209 while confirming
B172, B210–B211 from the resource reports in
Claytex and VeSyMA, B212 from WP8, and B213–B218 on 2026-09-18 from the decision that WP2 opens with
`analysis-viewer-fidelity.md` Part II, and B219–B223 on 2026-09-19 from the whole-solution
mutation audit, with B224 from what that audit did to this repository while running, B225–B226 from a second pass over the survivors asking which of them sit on code that writes to a user’s files, and B227–B229 as what that pass deliberately left for WP9, B230–B232 on 2026-09-19 from running WP2 step 1, B233 from the limit B214 shipped with, B234 from noticing that B213's strongest test runs nowhere automatically, B235 from measuring B185, B236 reported from ordinary use on 2026-09-19, B237 from a journey that could not be made reliable, B238–B250 on 2026-09-19/20 from WP2 and WP3, half from the work itself and half from a user exercising what it had just shipped, B251–B258 on 2026-09-20 from ordinary use and CI, B259–B260 on 2026-09-21 from using the external tool checks in WP5, and B261–B263 on 2026-09-21 from what B174's profiling left measured but unexplained and from the external tool interface changes in PR #9, B264 from doing B240 and finding the same assumption three paths wider, B265 from using the SVN history view on a real repository, B266 from what B265's own guards revealed about the suite filter, B267 on 2026-09-21 from WP7's mutation run refusing to start, B268 from tracing a Code Review defect back to a library index that claims classes it does not supply, B269-B270 on 2026-09-21 from one crash report: the diff viewer taking the application down, and what reloading after it did, B271 from the journey B237 exists to make possible, B272 from the tests B229 asked for finding a promise one of two paths had never kept, B273 on 2026-09-22 from the first test Of B1–B167 all are closed
except the two carried forward below, and the table they lived in was retired with the phase design
notes on 2026-09-17 — git history has it if the reasoning behind one of those ids is ever needed.

New items start at **B409**. The watermark moves as items close, not only as they are opened: the id
guard checks that the ids *above* it run unbroken, so a closed row leaves a gap the moment it is
removed unless the watermark has advanced past it.

`MLQT.Cli.Tests/MarkdownTableTests.cs` holds this file's table structure and the uniqueness of the
ids. A blank line between two rows silently ends the table, and an unescaped `|` in a cell splits it
even inside backticks — both have happened, and neither is visible in review.

## Carried forward from phases 7a / 7b

Two items whose id is already in use elsewhere and whose work is not finished.

| # | Item | From | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B152 ✅ | **Twelve documentation screenshots are still photographs** | doc review 2026-09-11 | ⭐ | M | 44 of the 56 images are regenerated from the real UI by `DocumentationScreenshots`; the rest cannot be produced that way and are listed in CLAUDE.md and `skill-gui-testing.md`. Two of them need Dymola, six need an SVN server, one needs an SVN repository to render its settings section, one needs the merge dialog's ready-to-merge phase (which needs a VCS operation performed *in the application* to refresh working-copy status), and the rest show the window frame. **Not a defect** — recorded so nobody re-derives the list. Worth revisiting only if a fixture ever gains an SVN server. **Mostly done 2026-09-27, closed**: eight of the twelve are now generated. An SVN repository needs no server, only `svnadmin create`, so `LibraryFixture` gains an SVN variant (`LibraryFixtureVcs.Svn`, a trunk checkout of a local `file://` repository with a branch and a tag), and `DocumentationScreenshots` uses it last for `svn-operations-1` to `-6` and `settings-reference-4`. `git-operations-6` is reached by committing from the merge dialog's own dirty phase, the in-application VCS operation that makes it re-read working-copy status. Each new image checked by eye against its caption; the full Journeys suite passes (76). The generator now needs svn and svnadmin. Still photographs, by nature: `code-review-4` and `code-review-5` (a Dymola check caught part way, and a finding only a failed Dymola check carries, which the fixture has no class to produce) and the window-frame shots. |
| B166 ✅ | **`check_library` once returned a different finding count on each run, and the variance has not reproduced** | 2026-09-17 | ⭐⭐ | M | The three causes found alongside it are fixed — the MCP call passed the wrong dictionary root, `modelsChecked` counted excluded libraries, and one `AnalyzeDependenciesAsync` call omitted the library roots so `modelica://` resolution depended on call order. The original 20,763 / 20,790 / 20,766 spread did **not** reproduce: three calls in one session and three with a full reload between them each returned 21,249. Two details weaken the original report — the reload path returns a **hardcoded** `affectedModelCount: 0`, so "no source change" was read off a literal; and the runs were scoped to `Modelica` alone inside a graph holding seven other libraries, which is a materially different shape. **Left open at low confidence.** If it moves again, capture the **per-rule breakdown of two adjacent runs** — a total says nothing about which rule moved. **Not reproduced 2026-09-27, closed**: the reported shape recreated with the real `SessionTools`/`StyleTools` in one process - the MSL repository's eight libraries plus Buildings, 15,343 classes - then `check_library` scoped to `Modelica` with every rule on, compared by per-rule count and by a hash of every finding (model, rule, line, summary, severity) rather than by total: 10 runs in one session, 5 with a full reload before each (settings from the repository, exercising the dictionary-root path, and passed in), and across four separate processes. All 30 agreed exactly: 6,487 classes checked, 31,839 findings, hash 63E0649A851A. The original spread was most likely one of the load-order and parallel-load races fixed since (B384, B387, B450 and others). |

## Phase 1 — issues found testing the new build

Opened 2026-09-17 from a pass over the Photino release on both platforms. Mostly UI, not
exclusively. Grouped by area; the ids are in the order they were written down.

### Findings and the Code Review page

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B176 ✅ | **No way to search the code being reviewed** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | Locating a parameter by name in a long class means scrolling. A search box over the rendered source, with match highlighting and next/previous, is what the page is missing to be usable on a real class. **Done**: a "Find in code" box beside the view buttons, a match counter, and up/down that step and wrap. The two halves search different things on purpose — the page finds the **lines**, over the text as the user reads it (tags stripped, entities decoded, so `<html>` finds the documentation string and `KEYWORD` finds nothing), and `CodeViewer` tints occurrences inside each token's own span, keeping its syntax colour. **The limit worth knowing**: a match spanning two tokens cannot be tinted, because the text is coloured token by token and `der(y)` is three spans — but the line is still found and stepped to, so it is reachable. No automated test covers the wiring between the two halves; that is B237. |
| B182 ✅ | **Line numbers for naming violations do not line up with the rendered code** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | A finding on a naming violation points at the wrong line in the viewer. The likely cause is that the finding's line is computed against the **stored** source while the viewer renders the **reformatted** source — the page reformats in order to colour it, so the two can differ by however many lines the formatter moved. Either the mapping has to go through the same representation the viewer shows, or the viewer has to stop reformatting. Establish which before fixing: the same mismatch would affect every rule whose line comes from a visitor, not only naming. **The viewer stopped reformatting (B215), so this closed as an identity rather than a fix**: a finding's line is counted against the class's own source, and that is now what is on screen. The guess in this row was right about the cause and right that it was not only naming — it was every rule. Measured before the change: only 3.7–5.8% of displayed lines were the file's line with the file's text on it. |
| B183 ✅ | **Findings do not scroll to their line, and arrive in no particular order** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Two halves. Clicking a finding should scroll the viewer so the line it is about is **in view** — currently a finding on a parameter can select a class whose relevant line is off screen. And the list order is whatever the check produced; it should be stable and sorted alphabetically, so the same library reviewed twice presents the same list twice. **Both halves done with B215.** The order is by class, then by line within it, then by rule — which is how a reader works through a class, and is why it is not alphabetical by message. The scroll is a new `spellCheck.scrollLineIntoView` beside the word-scroll interop the spelling findings already used, and it only became possible because the viewer now shows the class rather than a copy 17–24% longer than it. A finding inside a collapsed nested class scrolls to that class's marker rather than nowhere, which is `SourceElision.ToDisplayLine` answering with the nearest honest line. |
| B186 ✅ | **The findings table runs off the bottom of the page** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Part of the table is not reachable, particularly when each finding spans several lines. A splitter between the viewer and the findings list would let the user choose, but the row count shown has to vary with the space given to it as well, or resizing just moves which part is cut off. **Both halves done, and the row count does not vary — it is gone.** The table is virtualised and scrolled rather than paged, so every finding is reachable at any pane size and a real library's tens of thousands of them (26,557 for MSL) still cost only the rows on screen. The splitter is `MudExSplitPanel`, the control MainLayout already uses, turned the other way; the two fixed heights it replaces were `221px` for the table and `calc(100vh - 405px)` for the viewer, the second being the first plus 185 px for everything above it. `ResizablePanesJourney` drags it in a real browser and measures the pane, because that is the claim. |
| B187 ✅ | **The finding filter cannot filter by rule, and combines its terms with OR** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | Two defects in one control: there is no way to narrow to a rule type, and entering more than one term widens the result instead of narrowing it. It should be **AND** — a partial model name *and* a keyword — which is what anyone typing two things into one box expects. **Both done, and the order mattered**: the class scope was smuggled through the search string (`_searchString + " " + NavState.ModelID`, stripped back out inside the filter), so swapping OR for AND first would have excluded every finding whose text did not also contain the class name — which is all of them. The scope and the rule are parameters now, and `CodeReview.Matches` is a pure function with fourteen tests over it. The rule list offers only the rules the current findings use, by catalogue title: offering all forty-odd makes the control something to search rather than something to pick from. |
| B178 ✅ | **The diff view uses its own colours instead of the syntax scheme** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | Side-by-side diff markup does not follow the rendering colour scheme used when viewing a single file, so the same code is coloured two ways in two panes of the same page. The user's chosen preset should reach both. **Closed by the diff adopting `ModelicaTokenClassifier`** — it classifies each whole side once and indexes the result, rather than highlighting a line at a time, so the ~100 lines of keyword-list regex are gone. **The planning note's fix was not the one needed.** It said to share the *colour source* between two stylesheets, because the two highlighters "cannot be merged — a diff hunk is not parseable". Both halves turned out otherwise: the sides are whole documents, not hunks, so one classifier does serve both; and the preset already reached the diff, because the runtime stylesheet is global and every rule carries `!important`. What was actually wrong is that the regex could only produce four categories, so identifiers, types, names and functions were plain text in the diff and coloured in the viewer. Emitting all nine fixed it with no change to either stylesheet. |
| B185 ✅ | **A very large class can fail to render at all** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | An imported FMU — `Engines Examples I2 fmu` — was still not rendered after five minutes. Two candidate costs and they need separating before either is addressed: the syntax highlighting over a very large token stream, and the reformat the page performs in order to colour it. The likely answer is a size threshold above which the page shows plain source with no highlighting and no reformat, but measure which half costs the time first. Relates to B182, which is about the same reformat. **Measured, and it is neither half.** B215 removed the reformat; the highlighting costs 20–148 ms at every size tried. It is the **parse**, and not because of size — a run of comments inside an `equation` section makes it quadratic (**B235**), while 554 KB of annotated declarations parse in 367 ms. **The likely answer in this row was therefore wrong twice over**: there is no size to threshold on, and turning the highlighting off would have saved nothing. What ships instead is that the viewer stops *waiting*: above 64 KB the lexer paints the class at once and the tree's colouring replaces it when the parse lands, the difference being only whether an identifier is known to be a type or a call. The threshold is a judgement about when a stall would be noticed, not a prediction of one — said explicitly at the constant, because a number that looks like a prediction will be read as one. The cause is B235 and that is where the real fix is. |
| B213 ✅ | **A token classifier, so the viewer can colour the file instead of a rewrite of it** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | `ModelicaTokenClassifier` in `ModelicaParser`, beside `ModelicaRenderer`: walk the parse tree recording token index → category, then emit the **original** source verbatim with each token wrapped in the tag `CodeViewer` already consumes. Driven by character offsets, never by token text, so whatever the lexer skipped is copied through and the output is exact by construction. Three tiers — parse tree, lexer only, verbatim — so a class that does not parse is still coloured. Two property tests carry it: **round trip** (strip the tags, get the source back) over the fixture library, and **agreement** (same categories as `ModelicaRenderer` on already-formatted input). Measured at 99.98% / 99.77% agreement over 114,642 identifier tokens and byte-exact over 1.09M lines; see `analysis-viewer-fidelity.md` §4, §5, §9 and §14 S2. **Delivered** as `ModelicaParser/Visitors/ModelicaTokenClassifier.cs` with 31 tests. Round trip is asserted over a curated corpus always, and over **every `.mo` file in a real library** when `MLQT_FIDELITY_CORPUS` points at one — 8,367 files exact. Three corrections to the plan, all found by S0 and all in the walk rather than the emitter: the graphics level has **three** increment sites and the middle one is how a *named* argument's value gets one (that is what colours `DynamicSelect`); `der`/`initial`/`pure` are calls; and a dotted name is one tag to the renderer and one tag per IDENT here, so any comparison has to split it — but never inside a quoted identifier. **The tiering changed**: the lexer-only tier is chosen by the caller for a class too large to parse, not reached by failure, because the parser recovers rather than throwing. The one deliberate divergence from today's colours is B232. |
| B214 ✅ | **One range-elision mechanism, with a line map that inverts** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Hiding annotations, hiding a package's class definitions, the MCP annotation strip and the package trimmer are four spellings of the same operation, and each is currently the renderer simply not visiting a subtree. In a verbatim view each becomes a dropped range of source lines, optionally replaced by a one-line marker. `SourceElision` — ordered, non-overlapping ranges plus `ToSourceLine`/`ToDisplayLine` — is that operation once, with invertibility as a property test. **Not deferrable**: every package opens with class definitions hidden, so this is how a package is displayed at all. `analysis-viewer-fidelity.md` §11b. **Delivered** as `ModelicaParser/Helpers/SourceElision.cs` (the mechanism) and `ModelicaParser/Visitors/ElisionFinder.cs` (the tree reading), with 30 tests; invertibility is a theory over nine range shapes and the package-collapsing gate is asserted directly. Overlapping ranges are refused rather than merged — there is no single answer for what the display shows, and a caller that produced them has a bug. **One decision that is not in the plan and is a real limit**: a construct is elided **as a unit or not at all**, so an annotation sharing its first or last line with code is left visible rather than leaving `Real x "d" annotation (Placement(` on screen. In MSL that hides the class-level graphics annotations, which are written on their own lines and are 41–44% of all lines, and keeps the inline `Placement` ones. Doing the mid-line case properly needs markup-aware splicing and is **B233**. |
| B215 ✅ | **The Code Review page shows the file, not the renderer's rewrite of it** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Wire B213 and B214 into the page: the source rule (use the stored code while `SourceMatchesFile`, otherwise re-slice the file by `StartIndex`/`StopIndex` **with the decoder those offsets were recorded with**), the classifier in place of `ModelicaRenderer`, `ShowRawSource` deleted in favour of the lexer tier, and `PrependElementPrefix` inserting into verbatim text. **Closes B182, B183 and B185.** Measured: ~95% of displayed lines are currently not where the user's editor puts them, and the fidelity path is ~45% cheaper end to end. The page has no component tests today; they arrive with this. **Done, and it closed B182 and B183 with it — B185 is what is left** (the lexer-only tier is built and public but nothing chooses it yet, so the size threshold is still to come). `CodeReview.Show` is the whole of the new path: `ClassSource` for the text, `ModelicaTokenClassifier` for the colour, `SourceElision.Merge` for what is hidden, and the map kept beside the lines because one without the other cannot say which line of the class a displayed line is. **Three things went rather than being adapted**: `ShowRawSource` and the parser-error branch (the lexer tier colours what recovered, where the old path showed no colour at all), `LoadOriginalFileContents` orphaned with it, and `_modelicaCode`/`_lines`, which were written at three sites each and read nowhere. Ten tests on a page that had none; the one that matters asserts every displayed line equals the line the user wrote, over a deliberately loosely-formatted class the old path would have renormalised on its first declaration. |
| B216 ✅ | **`PackageCodeTrimmer` re-renders where it could excise** | Checking | ⭐⭐ | M | Trimming a package's inline standalone children runs them through `ModelicaRenderer`, so the checked text is a rewrite of the file and `SourceMatchesFile` goes false — which is why findings in those packages point at no line at all. Excising each child's `[StartIndex..StopIndex]` instead leaves a subsequence of the file, restores the line mapping, and improves the CLI and MCP reports as well as the viewer. **The riskiest change in the set**: the trimmer feeds the pipeline all three surfaces share, so the gate is the parity number (MSL = 34329) with no finding moving other than by the removed ranges. Two reassurances short of proof — the trimmer passes `FormattingOptions.None`, which reorders nothing, and no rule is sensitive to whitespace or line length. Decided by measurement before anything is built. **S1 decided it: in scope** — no finding is lost on either library, MSL gains 2 correct `OneOfEachSection` findings (excision leaves an empty section behind that re-rendering dropped, so it is baseline drift to declare), and the line map is exact over 30,599 lines. **Finish the file's mutation survivors with the rewrite.** Seven are left after B230 and each sits on a decision this item replaces or inherits: the selection rule at `:84` (both `&&` operands and the `"package"` literal), the three early returns at `:88`, `:96` and `:101` (nothing to exclude, the parse failed, a short class definition has no body), the parse-error guard at `:95`, and `excludeClassDefinitions: false` at `:106`. They have been in the WP8 audit report unclaimed since it ran; excision keeps the selection rule and the short-class case, and drops the rest with the render, so the honest end state is each one either killed by a test or gone with the code. **Done 2026-09-20.** The trimmer cuts the children's lines out instead of re-rendering the package, so what is left is the file's own text and `ModelNode.TrimElision` records which lines went - `ClassLocation.FileLine` takes the offset after putting them back. **Gate met on both libraries**: MSL 0 findings lost, exactly the 2 `OneOfEachSection` findings S1 predicted gained (and they are correct - `ReferenceAir.mo` really does have three public sections, which re-rendering merged), and **432 findings that used to be pinned to the class declaration now point at the line they are about**; Buildings 0 lost, 0 gained, 34 findings gained a line. Two things the item did not foresee. `SourceMatchesFile` was answering two questions - *do lines map* and *is this the whole class* - which the old trim made identical and this one separates, so `ClassSource` needed `StoredSourceIsWholeClass` or the diff view would have shown a trimmed package against itself. And the per-child file check had to be added: computing a child's range from a line number measured against a *different* file produced ranges that collided, and the whole trim was abandoned - B230's mistake one level down, caught by an existing test. **Survivors**: 3 of the 7 went with the render, 4 are killed by new tests, and the rest are recorded as judged-equivalent in the test file. One test written for them asserted nothing - a class named `package` cannot parse - and was removed rather than left. |
| B230 ✅ | **`PackageCodeTrimmer` re-renders packages that have nothing to trim** | Checking | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | A package whose standalone children are already in their own files has no inline child to remove — but the trimmer rendered it anyway, marked `SourceMatchesFile` false, and handed every later report a class whose lines were the renderer's rather than the file's. **377 of 688 trimmed packages in MSL (55%) and 1,172 of 1,235 in Buildings (95%) had nothing to remove**, and in Buildings 1,073 came out *longer* than the file. Fixed by one clause in the candidate filter: a child only counts if it shares the package's `ContainingFileId`. **Classes that lose their line mapping: MSL 688 → 311 (10.61% → 4.79%), Buildings 1,235 → 63 (16.44% → 0.84%)**, with the finding count unchanged on both (26,557 and 31,823) — a guard clause should move no finding, and it moves none. 174 MSL findings gain their true file line as a side effect; the remaining 654 are B216's. Sharing the file is the cheap half of the question — whether the child's `[StartIndex..StopIndex]` lies inside the package's is the exact one, and B216 asks it because it excises those ranges. `analysis-viewer-fidelity.md` §17.1. |
| B231 ✅ | **`ModelNode.StartIndex`/`StopIndex` are documented as something they are not** | Checking | ⭐⭐ | S | The field says "zero-based character offset ... in the underlying source file, read with `Encoding.Latin1` to match the parser" and that `StopIndex` is the last character, inclusive. Slicing a file that way reproduces **0 of 13,997 classes** across MSL and Buildings. They are offsets into the **line-ending-normalised** text `PreprocessCode` produces, and the slice that matches is `[Start..Stop+1]` — 99.49% / 98.38%, the residue being short class definitions, whose stored text carries a trailing `;` the slice does not. Every file in both libraries is CRLF, so a caller following the comment does not get a slightly wrong slice, it gets a different class. Correct the comment, and give the slice a helper so no caller has to know. `analysis-viewer-fidelity.md` §17.4. |
| B232 ✅ | **`ModelicaRenderer` colours array subscripts as function calls** | Code Review | ⭐ | S | `_isFunction` is a mutable field, not a scoped fact, and it shows in two ways. It **leaks into subscripts**: `VisitComponent_reference` visits the reference's `array_subscripts` while the flag is still set, so in `den2[i] := …` the subscript `i` is coloured as a call (377 tokens in MSL, 512 in Buildings). And a **nested call clears it** for the rest of the reference: in `m[integer(i),j] := …` the inner `integer(…)` resets it on the way out, so `j` loses the colouring the leak had given it (9 and 22). Found by the B213 agreement measurement, which is otherwise 99.998% — this is the entire residue. **The classifier should not reproduce either**, so closing this is mostly deciding that the visible change is wanted and saying so. **Done 2026-09-19.** `_isFunction` is saved and restored at each of the four sites that set it, and cleared around a reference's `array_subscripts`, which fixes both facets: a subscript is no longer a call, and a call nested in one no longer switches the colouring off for what follows. The classifier already declined to reproduce either, so the two now agree and `ASubscriptIsNotAFunctionCall_UnlikeTheRenderer` - which existed to record the divergence - is rewritten as `...AndTheRendererAgrees`. **Worth knowing before touching this again: the renderer's colouring is no longer what any user sees.** Every production call site passes `renderForCodeEditor: false` since B215; the tag-emitting mode survives only as the independent second implementation the classifier is measured against, which is the argument for fixing it rather than deleting it - a baseline with a known defect has to be explained every time it is used. Five tests, and the whole-library fidelity test re-run by hand (B234) since the renderer is that measurement's other side. |
| B235 ✅ | **A class with commented-out equations parses in quadratic time** | Parser | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | A run of comment lines inside an `equation` section makes `ModelicaParserHelper.ParseWithTokens` quadratic: **500 of them parse in 1.5 s, 1,000 in 4.4 s, 2,000 in 17 s, 4,000 in 69 s** — four times the work for twice the text, at only 323 KB. The same class with the comments removed parses in 86 ms, and 4,000 *annotated* declarations (554 KB, half as many lines again) parse in 367 ms, so it is neither size nor volume. **The input is legal Modelica and parses with zero errors**, so this is the parser's prediction rather than its error recovery — `equation_or_comment : (c_comment \| (equation ';'))` with `COMMENT` and `LINE_COMMENT` on the default channel is where to look, and `statement_or_comment` is the same shape. **This is not a viewer problem**: the check pipeline parses every class, so a library with commented-out equations costs the CLI and the MCP server the same minutes it costs the page. B185 only stopped the viewer *waiting* for it. Reproduce with `MLQT_LARGE_CLASS_COST=1` over `LargeClassCostTests`, which measures three shapes so the cause cannot be misattributed again — the first version of that measurement varied the annotations and the comments together and blamed the annotations. **✅ Done 2026-09-20, and it is not what the row named.** Reproduced first, varying one thing at a time, and the fourth shape gave it away: the **same comments followed by one real equation** parse in 22ms where the same comments at the end of the section take 16,813ms - **764×**. So it is not comments, and not `equation_or_comment`; it is a **trailing run** of comments in any `(c_comment \| X)*` loop, where the parser must scan the whole run at every comment to decide whether the loop has ended. `element_list` had it too, at 17,933ms for 2,000, which is why the fix covers all three loops rather than only the reported one. **The fix is one character in each**: `c_comment` → `c_comment+`, so a run is consumed in one iteration and the ambiguous decision is taken once rather than once per comment. Measured: 2,000 comments in an equation section **16,813ms → 79ms**, in an element list **17,933ms → 81ms**, both now linear; `plain` and `annotated` unchanged. **The gates, all of them**: 8,367 real files rendered before and after with **0 differing** - the write path is byte-identical - the classifier's whole-library round trip still exact, and MSL 5,250 / Buildings 3,285 findings unchanged. Four tests, asserting **growth rather than a threshold** because a millisecond count is a property of the machine and 4×-the-work-for-2×-the-text is a property of the parser. Reverting the grammar alone does not build - the call sites take an array now - so the coupling is its own guard. |
| B237 ✅ | **Nothing automated proves the code search is wired up** | Testing | ⭐ | S | B176's two halves are unit-tested — which lines match (`CodeReviewCodeSearchTests`) and what the markup looks like once tinted — but the wiring between them is not: the term travels from the field through `CodeReview` to `CodeViewer`, which re-renders only when it notices the parameter changed, and only a browser can say whether that happens. **A journey for it was written and then removed**, because it could not be made reliable in the shared host: searching needs a class selected, and the two ways to get one both fail there. Through the tree needs a *repository*, and registering one starts the analysis pipeline and leaves it registered — which failed the journey and then timed out `TabNavigationJourney` five minutes later. Clicking an injected finding avoids all that and **passes in isolation in 11 s**, but in a full run the code viewer never renders at all, for a reason in the shared host that was not found. The feature was verified by that isolated run and by the regenerated screenshot. Worth either a journey with a host of its own, or understanding why a class will not open when the other journeys have run first — the second is the more useful answer, because `ResizablePanesJourney` will hit it too the moment it needs a class. **Half of it is found and fixed, and the journey is still not shipped (2026-09-21).** The shared host had a cause nobody had named: every journey builds its own `LibraryFixture` under a fresh temp path and **every one of them is called `Lib`**, so they all produce the same class ids - and nothing ever took a library back out of the shared `ILibraryDataService`. `LibraryFixture.Dispose` deletes the directory and leaves the library registered, and `DirectedGraph.AddNode` keeps the copy that arrived first, so `Lib.Modified` resolved to the first journey's node whose file had been deleted with its fixture. That is why a class opens alone and never renders in a full run, and `ResizablePanesJourney` would have met it the moment it needed one. `TestHostFixture.ResetLibrariesAsync` now empties the graph at the **start** of each journey - at the start rather than the end, because a journey that fails half way through does not get to clean up. All 71 journeys pass with it. **It was not the whole cause.** The journey was written again (four tests: tinting appears, the count fills, clearing removes it, a term that matches nothing changes nothing) and it *still* passes alone and fails beside its own siblings - so it is not committed, because a test that depends on what ran before it is worse than no test. What is left is not about libraries: the circuit terminates with `InvalidOperationException: The current thread is not associated with the Dispatcher` out of `CodeReview.OnModelSelected`, which is **B271**. Fix that first; this closes behind it. **✅ Closed 2026-09-21, and the reason was found rather than worked around.** Two causes, both in the harness. The first: every journey's fixture library is called `Lib`, so they all produce the same class ids, and **nothing ever took a library back out of the shared service** - `LibraryFixture.Dispose` deletes the directory and leaves the library registered, and `AddNode` keeps the copy that arrived first, so a class resolved to the first journey's node whose file had been deleted with its fixture. `TestHostFixture.ResetLibrariesAsync` empties the graph at the **start** of each journey, because one that fails half way through does not get to clean up. The second, and the one the row could not name: **no journey ever closed a page**, and every open page is a live circuit while `AppState` is a singleton shared by all of them - so a page left open went on reacting to events raised by whatever page came after it, `CodeReview.OnModelSelected` ran on the raising circuit's dispatcher rather than its own, and the render call killed circuits no test was looking at, taking the current one's code viewer with them. `NewPageAsync` now closes the page it handed out last. **The desktop host has exactly one circuit, so none of it was reachable in the product** - which is why the fix is here and not in a component made to tolerate it. `CodeSearchJourney` is committed: four tests over the wiring B176 left untested - the tint appears, the count fills, clearing removes it, a term matching nothing changes nothing. **The suite is faster for it**: 75 tests in 59s where 71 took 2m05s, because the leaked circuits were costing time as well as reliability. |
| B238 ✅ | **A rule switched on while its prerequisite is off is lost on the next save** | Settings | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Found while doing B181, and it predates it. The four rules with a prerequisite were serialized through a bool facade whose getter answers the *effective* question - will this rule run - which is false while the prerequisite is off. So a rule the user had switched on was written as `false`, and because the facade's setter removes the map entry when given false, reading the file back deleted the entry `RuleSeverities` ahead of it had just restored. A repository that turned `OneOfEachSection` off lost its ordering rules at the next save, silently, while the dialog went on showing them ticked - it binds to `IsRuleSwitchedOn` precisely so an inert rule still reads as configured, which is what hid it. The bool reconciliation was built to be order-independent and is, for `true`; the asymmetry is that disabling removes unconditionally, right for a caller saying "off" and wrong for a redundant `false` that only means "not currently in effect". **Fixed 2026-09-19**: the legacy JSON name carries the configured value and the effective facade is out of the JSON surface, so old settings files still load and no C# caller changes meaning. Six tests, driven off `RuleCatalog` so a fifth rule with a prerequisite is covered the day it is added. |
| B239 ✅ | **Nothing enforced the rule that every Modelica file read and write goes through `ModelicaFileEncoding`** | Testing | ⭐⭐ | S | Raised while reviewing B236: the funnel held because people remembered, not because anything failed if they did not. That is the shape this backlog keeps naming - a promise in CLAUDE.md with no test behind it - and B236 is what it looks like when it goes wrong, two write paths disagreeing for the whole life of the project. **Done 2026-09-20**: `ModelicaFileAccessPolicyTests` scans production source for raw file-text APIs and requires each to be in a ledger with a reason. It cannot tell what a `path` variable holds, so it does not guess - it puts the judgement in front of a human once and keeps it visible, the same trade `CodeBehindPolicyTests` makes. Counts are part of each entry, so a second call in an already-listed file is not covered by the first one's reason. Verified by mutation: pointing the incremental formatter at `File.WriteAllTextAsync` fails it. It found two sites a manual grep had missed and one real gap (B240), and the picker's Modelica entry point now uses the funnel. |
| B240 ✅ | **A conflicted file's `.mine`/`.rN` sidecars are read outside the encoding funnel** | Revision control | ⭐ | S | Found by B239's scan. `SvnRevisionControlSystem.GetConflictVersions` reads the two sidecar files SVN leaves beside a conflicted file with `File.ReadAllText`, which is a UTF-8 decode - so for a Windows-1252 library the conflict diff shows replacement characters where the accented characters and curly quotes are. **Read-only and display-only**: it feeds `ConflictDiffDialog` and nothing writes the result back, so the cost is a diff that looks wrong rather than a file that becomes wrong. **The fix is not simply to call the funnel.** `RevisionControl` has no project references at all - it is deliberately Modelica-agnostic - so reaching `ModelicaFileEncoding` means either a new dependency, which gives up that property, or passing a decoder in from the caller. Worth deciding rather than doing. **✅ Done 2026-09-21, and the decision went the other way from both options.** Neither a new project reference nor a decoder passed in: **`GetConflictVersions` returns the bytes it was given** and MLQT decodes them in `MLQT.Services/Helpers/VcsFileText`, through `ModelicaFileEncoding.DetectFromBytes`. A version control system stores bytes; what they mean is the caller's question, so the assembly that knows nothing about Modelica does not have to learn anything to stop getting this wrong. **Git had the same defect and the row did not say so** - `Blob.GetContentText()` decodes as UTF-8 exactly as `File.ReadAllText` does, and B239's scan could not see it because it was looking for raw file APIs. Both are fixed; fixing the reported one and leaving its sibling is the shape that produced B170's second and third reports. Five tests, one of which asserts the property that forced the shape - that `RevisionControl` still references none of our projects - because the tempting fix is the one that quietly breaks it. **The ledger entry in `ModelicaFileAccessPolicyTests` now says the reads are deliberate byte reads**; left alone it would have gone on describing the defect as current. **What this did not fix is B264**: the same UTF-8 assumption in `GetFileContentAtRevision` and in `SvnCli`'s stdout. |
| B264 ✅ | **Everything read out of VCS history is decoded as UTF-8, not just the conflict sidecars** | Revision control | ⭐⭐ | M | Found while doing B240, which fixed one path of several. `GitRevisionControlSystem.GetFileContentAtRevision` wraps the blob in `new StreamReader(stream)`, whose default is UTF-8, and `SvnCli` sets `StandardOutputEncoding = Encoding.UTF8`, so `svn cat` of a Windows-1252 file comes back with replacement characters before any MLQT code sees it. **It feeds the revision diff B202 just rebuilt** and the Code Review diff, so an accented library shows mojibake on both sides of a comparison of itself. **B240's answer applies and was deliberately not applied blind**: return the bytes and let `VcsFileText` decode them. It is a bigger change than the sidecars were - `GetFileContentAtRevision` hands a string to several callers, and the SVN half needs `SvnCli` to capture stdout as bytes instead of decoding it on the way out, which affects every command that reads output. Read-only, like B240: the cost is a diff that looks wrong, not a file that becomes wrong. **✅ Done 2026-09-21, the same way B240 went.** `GetFileContentAtRevision` is now `GetFileBytesAtRevision`: the VCS layer hands back what was stored and `VcsFileText` decodes it at the service boundary, through the same funnel a file on disk goes through. Git reads the blob's bytes instead of wrapping it in a default `StreamReader`; SVN gained `SvnCli.RunForBytes`, used by **`svn cat` and nothing else** - every other command writes text svn generated itself, which is UTF-8 by definition, so decoding that is right. **The ~50 tests that assert on text were not rewritten**: an extension method in the test project decodes for them, which is harmless where the fixtures are ASCII and is deliberately not offered on the production type, because a string-returning sibling there is what every caller would reach for. Seven tests, covering all three SVN routes (BASE, pegged revision, and by URL with the file gone from disk) - **mutation found that the first version only exercised the BASE route**, which is not the one the history diff takes. The Git entry in `ModelicaFileAccessPolicyTests`' ledger is gone, because the raw stream read it accounted for no longer exists. **What could not be demonstrated**: 45,929 `.mo` files across both local corpora - the SVN libraries and `C:\Projects\Modelica` - are all valid UTF-8, so no file on this machine reads differently before and after. The proof is at unit level, on files written as Windows-1252 by the tests themselves. |
| B265 ✅ | **Every SVN history diff failed to load the file** | Revision control | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Reported from a real repository: viewing r39803 of `ModelicaLibrariesTrunk` and clicking any changed file gave *“Could not load file content for 'trunk/Modelica/Claytex/Electrical/CurrentControl.mo' at 39803”*. **Not caused by B202 - B202 changed the wording of the failure.** Two path spaces reach the VCS layer and nothing said which was which: `svn log` reports repository-root-relative paths (`trunk/Modelica/Foo.mo`), everything working from the checkout uses working-copy-relative ones. The dialog converted between them by stripping `trunk/` and testing whether the result was on disk - **against `repository.LocalPath`, while the lookup underneath used `repository.VcsRootPath`**. Those are different directories whenever a repository is registered at a library *inside* the checkout, which is how this one is registered (`...\ModelicaLibrariesTrunk\Modelica` against `...\ModelicaLibrariesTrunk`), so the strip never fired and `svn cat` ran against a path that could not exist. **The guess is gone**: when the working copy cannot answer, the SVN system asks the server - repository root first, then the working copy's own URL - which also fixes a revision **newer than the working copy** and a file deleted since, neither of which any amount of prefix-stripping could have reached. Verified against the reported repository and revision: the file now loads, and a revision from before it existed still returns nothing rather than the wrong content. **And the revision genuinely changed no lines** - r39803 is a merge whose change to that file is `svn:mergeinfo` only, which is why the commit lists dozens of untouched directories, so the empty-diff message now says that rather than leaving the user looking at a list that disagrees with it. Seven tests over the URL building, which is where the mistakes are (a doubled slash, a dropped branch, a library called `VeSyMA - Suspensions`); the server half stays hand-verified. |
| B266 ✅ | **281 SVN tests run nowhere, and one of them is failing** | Test fidelity | ⭐⭐ | M | Found while adding B265's guards, which the filter would have excluded. `run-all-tests.ps1` and CI run `RevisionControl.Tests` with `FullyQualifiedName!~Svn` because the SVN integration tests need a working copy and a server no runner has. It is a **substring** filter: the suite has 672 tests and 391 run, so **281 never execute anywhere**, and only two of the nine SVN test classes actually reference the repository-availability guard (`SvnIntegrationAdvancedTests`, 84 tests, and `SvnMergeCommitTests`, 6). The rest - `SvnCliTests`, `SvnOperationsTests`' filesystem-only conflict region, `SvnRevisionControlSystemTests`, `SvnToolLocatorTests`, `SvnChangedFilesSinceTests` - are excluded by their names alone. **And one excluded test is red**: `SvnIntegrationAdvancedTests.Commit_NestedDirectories_ReturnsSuccess` fails on the development machine, verified as pre-existing against an earlier commit in a worktree, which is B116's shape in a second suite - a red line nobody sees because the category is excused. The work is to classify by what a test *needs* rather than what it is called, which is a decision with real CI risk attached: a runner has no svn client, so a test that merely shells out fails there even though it needs no server. Do it by moving the server-dependent classes behind one explicit marker and running the rest, one class at a time, on a runner. **✅ Done 2026-09-21, by measurement rather than by reading the names.** With svn taken off PATH, exactly three classes fail - `SvnIntegrationTests`, `SvnIntegrationAdvancedTests`, `SvnMergeCommitTests` - and every other test passes, so the filter now names those three and nothing else. **157 more tests run in CI** (548 against 391, verified by running the suite in the runner's configuration: no svn on PATH, new filter, 548 passed). `run-all-tests.ps1` applies the filter **only when the machine has no svn**, so a developer's run covers all 673. The four copies of the filter are held together by `MLQT.Shared.Tests/SvnTestFilterTests.cs`, which also fails if the substring form comes back, and `WorkflowPlatformParityTests` now takes the string from it rather than writing it out again - four copies of a rule with nothing holding them together is what let this happen. **The hidden failure was a test, not the product**: `Commit_NestedDirectories_ReturnsSuccess` committed `level1/level2/level3/DeepFile.txt` to trunk on some earlier run, so every run since found it already versioned with identical content, staged nothing, and failed. The path is unique per run now. **It cost an hour to diagnose because the message was wrong**: a commit that succeeds and commits nothing reported *“SVN commit failed”*, so there was no error anywhere to find - it now says nothing was committed and why, with a test. |
| B241 ✅ | **`MLQT.Structure.SingleFilePackage` defaults to Warning** | Rules | ⭐⭐ | S | Raised by the user on 2026-09-20, from the question of what happens when another tool saves a new package as one file inside an existing repository: MLQT never restructures it on its own - the incremental path reformats it in place and leaves it single-file - so the only signal is this rule, and a rule nobody has heard of is a rule nobody turns on. **Done**: the first rule in MLQT that is on unless a repository turns it off. `RuleDefinition.EnabledByDefault` carries it, and the interesting part is that *absent* now has to mean two different things - so a default-on rule stores `"Off"` rather than removing its key, has no bool facade in the settings file (a bool cannot say "not configured", and writing one added a key to every file that was merely read and written back), and `SetRuleEnabled(true)` treats a stored Off as absent or switching it back on would find the key and leave it Off. **17 tests across four suites asserted that a fresh settings object has nothing enabled**, which is a fair measure of how load-bearing that was; `StyleCheckingSettings.NothingEnabled()` gives the idea a name. `mlqt check` gained a third state between "no rules" and "configured": *only the rules that are on by default*, because one rule's worth of findings reads like a clean bill of health. |
| B242 ✅ | **Split into files: a fix for a single-file package, from the finding** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | M | Asked for on 2026-09-20 alongside B241: telling a user their package is stored wrongly and offering them **Format All Files** means reformatting thousands of files to correct one. `PackageSplitter` writes the package the finding names as a directory with a file per class and a matching `package.order`, deletes the file it came from, and touches nothing else - the parent's `package.order` already names it and still does. Offered on the finding's row and in the Finding Details dialog, and it asks before it writes. **What it acts on is what the rule reported**: both ask `SingleFilePackageAnalyzer.SplittableChildren`, so a package the rule did not report cannot be split and one it did moves exactly the classes the message named - the alternative being two copies of one decision, which is a shape this backlog has named four times. Two things came out of chasing its coverage: a package stored as `Sub/package.mo` with inline classes **is** reported by the rule and was being refused by the fix, which is offering a button that declines; and the case where the old file cannot be deleted now says the library holds the package twice rather than reporting success. 19 tests, against real files, including one that reloads the split library from scratch and compares the class list. |
| B243 ✅ | **Split into files destroyed a file when the package was nested in someone else's** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Reported from real use on 2026-09-20, on MSL. Splitting `Modelica.Electrical.Spice3.Internal.Jfet` wrote Jfet into the Spice3 directory instead of Internal's, then **deleted the file it came from** - `Spice3.mo`, holding twenty-two other packages - so Internal disappeared from the library browser and Jfet was nowhere. **Two causes.** B177's rule reported every package nested inside a single-file package, not just the one that owns the file: 302 findings over 71 files on MSL, 23 of them for `Spice3.mo` alone, and only one of those 23 could actually be fixed - a nested package has no file of its own and cannot be given a directory until its parent has one. And B242's splitter took the directory of the file it found the package in, wrote there, and then deleted that file because what it had written was somewhere else. **Fixed on both sides**: the rule reports only the class that owns its file (65 findings, one per file, every one splittable), and the splitter refuses to delete a file that still holds a class it did not write - checked by removing the rule fix and watching the file survive. Eight tests on the exact shape, including one that asserts the *in-place* graph update matches a fresh load, because what the user saw was the library browser and not the disk. Nothing covered a nested package before, which is why a full suite passed over a fix that could delete a user's work. |
| B244 ✅ | **The rule missed a partly split package, and a saved settings file did not record it** | Rules | ⭐⭐ | S | Two things reported together on 2026-09-20 from opening MSL. **The rule said nothing about `Spice3/Internal/package.mo`**, which keeps eight classes inline beside thirteen siblings that already have their own files. B177 read the rule as *entirely in one file, or say nothing*, on the grounds that a part-split package was a different situation; a real library shows that half-way through is the ordinary shape of the drift, and silence is how a class stays put for years. It now reports every class that could have a file and does not - 74 findings on MSL where the whole-file reading gave 65. **And a settings file written before the rule existed never gained it**: pressing Apply wrote nothing, because absent already meant on. That hides a gate from everyone reading the repository and lets it move under them on an upgrade, so saving a repository's settings now records every default-on rule at the severity it is running with - only adding, never overwriting a decision. One consequence of the default worth knowing: a rule that runs in *every* check has to survive a broken node, or one unreadable class costs the whole library its analysis rather than costing itself. |
| B245 ✅ | **Two children whose names differ only in case are both kept inline, though only one would be a file** | Rules / formatting | ⭐ | S | Why MSL's `Spice3.Internal.JFET` is still in package.mo, which took a probe to work out. It has a sibling *package* called `Jfet`, and `ModelicaPackageSaver` refuses to store two children as separate entries when their names differ only in case - so the formatter keeps both inline, and `MLQT.Structure.SingleFilePackage` deliberately says nothing, because reporting them would offer a fix that moves nothing. **The rule is coarser than the filesystem is.** A package becomes a directory `Jfet` and a model becomes `JFET.mo`; those cannot collide. Comparing the entries that would actually be written - `Name.mo` against `Name/`, case-insensitively - would let all four of MSL's pairs split. It is a change to the **write** path, so it wants the parity run and a full-library save compared before and after, which is why it is written down rather than taken. Four pairs in MSL alone: MOS/Mos, MOS2/Mos2, DIODE/Diode, JFET/Jfet. **Fixed 2026-09-22.** The comparison is now on the directory entry each child would be written as - `Name` for a package, `Name.mo` for everything else - and the answer lives once, in `ModelicaGraph/PackageFileLayout.cs`. That second part was the larger half of the row: `SingleFilePackageAnalyzer` had its own copy of the rule, commented *same rule ModelicaPackageSaver writes by*, and it was the same wrong answer twice, which is exactly why the rule could stay quiet about the pairs and nobody noticed the refusal was unnecessary. The reserved-name check went the same way round and is fixed with it: it refused any child called `package`, though a *package* of that name is written as a directory and collides with nothing - `Modelica.Icons.Package` is a real instance. The short-class question is asked lazily, only of siblings whose bare names collide, so the analyzer does not start parsing classes it never parsed before. **Gate run on MSL** (6,489 classes): nothing lost, 54 paths gained - the four pairs and `Modelica/Icons/Package/` - and 6,382 of the 6,384 common files byte-identical, the two that moved being the `package.mo` files that gave up children. Parity over a nine-rule check: 5,296 findings before and after, one message changed, `Modelica.Icons` 31 classes to 32. |
| B246 ✅ | **`MLQT.Structure.UsesUndeclared` reports Modelica's own built-ins and annotation graphics** | Rules | ⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-20: FluidPower is told that library `Line` is referenced and not declared in its `uses(...)`, and `Line` appears nowhere in its package.mo - **every library reports this**, along with `Connections`, `ExternalObject` and `rooted`. Two distinct gaps, both confirmed by reading `UsesHygieneAnalyzer`, which has no notion of either. `Connections`, `ExternalObject` and `rooted` are **Modelica language built-ins**, not libraries, and no `uses(...)` can ever declare them. `Line` is a **graphical primitive inside an annotation** - `Line`, `Rectangle`, `Polygon`, `Text`, `Ellipse`, `Bitmap`, `Placement` and the rest - so the analyzer is treating annotation content as a reference to a library. The second is the bigger one: it is why *every* library reports it, and it means the rule is currently unusable. Fixing it wants a list of the language's own names (the specification has one) and for annotation subtrees to be excluded from reference collection. Check what the sibling rules do with the same names before assuming this is only the uses rule. **Fixed 2026-09-22.** Neither gap was in the analyzer: both are in how a reference is collected, which is why the rule could not see them. `ModelicaLanguage` is the language's own names written down once - predefined types, operators, `Connections` and unqualified `rooted` - and `ReferenceResolver` checks it *before* looking a name up, so a library that happens to hold a class called `rooted` no longer collects an edge to it from every model that calls the operator. **Annotation content is no longer collected as references at all**: `Line` and `Rectangle` are annotation grammar, and so are `Icon`, `Dialog` and `Placement`, none of which resolution can tell from a class. The exception is `derivative`, `inverse` and `choices`, which do name classes - a blanket exclusion would have reported every `_der` function as unused. Two things fell out that were not in this row. The `uses(...)` annotation was itself counted as a reference to every library it declares, so **`MLQT.Structure.UsesDeclaredUnused` could never fire on a root package** - the rule was dead, not quiet. And the built-in list compared case-insensitively though Modelica is case-sensitive, so `Modelica.Blocks.Math.Sum`, `Max`, `Abs`, `Sign`, `Sqrt`, `Sin`, `Cos`, `Exp` and `Log` were dropped from the graph wherever a component was typed by their simple name. The sibling check found three copies of the predefined-type list, each different; they now derive from one, with `TypeResolver`'s extra `Complex` stated as the difference it is. |
| B247 ✅ | **The findings table does not say how many findings it is showing** | Code Review | ⭐ | S | With a search term, a rule filter and a scope all narrowing the list (B187), the number on screen is the only thing that says what the filters did - and there is none. A user cannot tell a filter that matched three findings from one that matched nothing but left the table looking plausible. Show the displayed count, and the total when they differ. Small, and it makes every other filter on that toolbar legible. **Four filters, not three**: the baseline toggle is one as well, and it was the only one the heading already accounted for. The heading now counts with `FilterFunc1`, the same predicate the table filters by, so the number above the rows cannot drift from the number of rows - which is the defect, stated precisely: the heading was computed from one set of findings while the table was filtered from another. Six wordings, in a pure `FindingsHeadingText` with its own tests, because the interesting part is which one applies and not the arithmetic. **The one judgement worth recording**: with the baseline toggle on, the denominator is the toggle's own count rather than the ledger - `2 of 7 changed findings`, not `2 of 40` - because the latter credits the search box with hiding the 33 the toggle hid, which is B247 again one level in. Three journey tests for the wiring, which no unit test can see, verified by putting the old count back and watching all three fail. **A blur is needed to drive that search box**: it is a plain `@bind-Value` with no `Immediate`, so filling it and reading the page straight away shows everything unchanged - true, and not the fault under test. `code-review.md` was stale on two counts beside this and is corrected: it described **two** filters where there are four, and said search terms are "matched independently" when B187 made them AND. |

That is B249 reintroduced by the fix for B248, caught by B249's own test. |

| B250 ✅ | **The Code Review page scrolls as a whole, including the class name box** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-20: a vertical scrollbar appears for the entire page, so the current-class text box at the top scrolls away. Nothing on this page should scroll except the regions that declare they do - the code viewer and the findings table - which is the same shape as the double scrollbar fixed earlier in the findings pane, one level out. **Exactly that shape, and the row's guess at the cause was wrong**: not a panel being resized, but a height that never had room to be right. The viewer's splitter was `calc(100vh - 185px)` and measured in the browser its top sits at 173px, so the page came to `100vh - 12px`. **12px is not a margin, it is the absence of one.** The parse-error alert above it is 34px, so a class with a syntax error made the page taller than the window - and the first thing a page-level scrollbar carries out of view is the box saying which class you are looking at. The constant was a hand-count of five things nobody controls: the app bar, the pane margins, the current-class box, the tab header and the page's own toolbar. **Three pages had three different numbers for the same distance** - 185 here, 140 on Dependencies, 135 on External Resources, plus 210 for the resource splitter - which is the tell. So the chain from the tab pane down to a page is now a flex column and each page fills what is left, `min-height: 0` at every step, panels scrolling rather than clipping; the four constants are gone. The same fix as `.mlqt-findings-pane` one level out, and its note already said why: *a flex calculation rather than a subtraction, because the height is not a constant*. **Measured before and after, not read**: the tab host's stack was already 676px inside a 668px parent, 8px over, hidden only because `MudExSplitPanelItem` has `overflow: auto`. Three journey tests, verified by reverting the fix and inserting the real alert - unfixed the viewer stays at 535px and the page grows, fixed it gives the 34px up. Two DOM facts cost a round each and are worth knowing: `MudStack` renders as `d-flex flex-column` with **no** `mud-stack` class, and `MudExSplitPanel` renders a grid wrapper around the panel so styling one leaves the other sizing to its content. All 65 journeys pass and 28 screenshots were regenerated. |
| B251 ✅ | **Formatting rewrote every file's line endings, so a whole library showed as modified with nothing in the diff** | Rules / formatting | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-20: **Format All Files** made every file in the library modified while `git diff` reported no differences at all, and the user's diagnosis - a line-ending change - was right. On a Windows checkout `core.autocrlf=true` hands you CRLF and stores LF, so a file MLQT rewrote as LF cleaned back to LF and the command line saw nothing, while **LibGit2Sharp**, which is what MLQT's own status reads, compared the bytes and called all 5,682 of them modified. **Structural, not accidental**: `ModelicaRenderer` joins its output with a line feed and nothing has ever converted it back, so every save path has always written LF whatever the file was. The answer belongs where the encoding's does and for the same reason - MLQT deciding how a user's file should look instead of writing it back the way it found it - so `ModelicaFileEncoding` now preserves the file's endings too, majority wins on a mixed file, and a new file keeps whatever its text came with rather than taking `Environment.NewLine`. That last was a second bug of the same kind: `WriteAllLines` used the platform's newline, so the same `package.order` came out CRLF on Windows and LF on Linux. **Measured**: formatting MSL twice now changes 1 file of 5,682, and that one is a file with no final newline gaining one (B236). Ten tests on the endings and one on the property a user meets - an already formatted CRLF library is byte-identical after formatting again. |
| B252 ✅ | **Components before classes does not order constants, parameters, variables and components** | Rules / formatting | ⭐⭐ | M | Asked for on 2026-09-20 after using B181: the expectation is **constants, parameters, variables, components, then classes**, and what the rule actually enforces is only the last boundary. It asks `element.class_definition()` against `element.component_clause()` and nothing else, so the four kinds of declaration can appear in any order among themselves - and `ModelicaRenderer` writes them as one undifferentiated `Element.Components` group, so the formatter could not produce the finer order either. **Two different problems behind one request.** `constant` and `parameter` come straight from `type_prefix` and are a syntactic question. **Telling a variable from a component is not**: `Real x` is a variable and `Resistor r` is a component, but so is `SI.Length x` a variable by every convention while its type is a class - so the declared type has to be followed through its alias chain to a simple type or not, which is exactly what `MissingUnits` already does and is the precedent to copy. **Decide the order before building it**: some teams write parameters before constants, and connectors conventionally group `input`/`output` together, so whether this is one fixed order or a configured one is the first question. Whatever it is, the formatter has to be able to produce it or the finding cannot be cleared - which makes this a **write-path** change carrying WP3's gate, a full-library save compared before and after. **Done 2026-09-22.** The order was settled first, as the row asks: **one fixed order** - inputs and outputs (as one group, which is how a function's signature and a connector's causal members read), constants, parameters, variables, components - and **not configurable**, because the formatter has to be able to produce whatever the rule asks for and one order it can always produce is worth more than a setting almost nobody would change. `MLQT.Style.DeclarationOrder` is a **new rule** rather than a redefinition of `ComponentsBeforeClasses`: a repository that had the coarse rule on must not silently acquire 3,246 new findings and a baseline full of drift. It takes the same prerequisite chain the renderer's nesting already has - declaration-order requires components-before-classes requires imports-first requires one-of-each-section. `DeclarationKinds` is the classifier both the rule and `ModelicaRenderer` ask, and **the renderer is given the same type lookup the checker is**, which is what stops the two ordering a class differently; it tracks the class it is inside so a nested class is resolved in its own scope, as the rule resolves it. Without a graph both recognise only the predefined types and leave everything else where a component goes - the same answer on both sides, which is the property that matters. **Gate, on MSL:** with the option off, all 6,438 written files are byte-identical to the previous build; with it on, 724 files are rewritten, and the check over the formatted tree reports **0 findings** where the library as shipped reports 3,246 - every finding the rule raises is one the formatter clears. |
| B253 ✅ | **Exclude from formatting takes seconds to come back** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-20: about ten seconds on a 172-line file, where it used to be instant. B175 changed the button from adding a name to a settings list into writing an annotation into the source, so it now writes, re-parses and reloads a file - but **the log says that is not where the time goes**. For `Blocks.Continuous.LimIntegrator` the write, the reload and the monitor restart are all inside one millisecond at 11:21:06.0565, and the re-render does not begin until 11:21:12.83: **6.8 seconds with nothing logged at all**, and 11.8 seconds for the previous file. **One redundant call is removed on the strength of that**: the toggle ran `CheckModelVcsStatus()` synchronously on the UI thread, which asks the VCS for the whole working copy, while every other path on the page runs it through `Task.Run(...).ContinueWith(...)` for exactly that reason - and `OnModelSelected()`, called on the next line, already does. **Not yet proved to be the whole of it**: the working-copy answer is cached for five minutes and was warm 50 seconds earlier at 50ms, so something else may be in that gap - `CaptureScrollForReloadAsync`'s JS interop and `ModelContentChanged` are the other candidates. The toggle is now timed per step, so the next click says which. **Found on the third attempt, and only by measuring.** The first two explanations were wrong and are recorded because the pattern is the lesson: a redundant synchronous `CheckModelVcsStatus()` (removed, correctly, but not the cause) and then the scroll-position interop (bounded at 250ms, worth doing, and it turned out to take 117ms). **The eleven seconds was `BaselineStatusService`**. It follows file activity so its answer stays current, and MLQT's own writes are file activity: saving the annotation restarts the monitor and calls `NotifyFileActivity`, which raises the event **synchronously**. The refresh asks every repository for its working-copy status - a scan of thousands of files on MSL - and the throttle took its *leading* edge on the caller's thread while queueing everything inside the window. That is the whole of the reported pattern: the first click after an idle spell paid for the scan, the next two seconds later took the queued path and cost a sixth of a second. It now runs on its own thread like the trailing refresh, which nothing needed it not to - `OnChanged` is how a view learns the snapshot moved and fires either way. Two tests, verified by putting the synchronous call back and watching the notification take 2,008ms; the first version of them passed vacuously because a service with no libraries returns before it queries anything. The per-step timing said `write annotation: 11164ms` on the first click and 151ms and 161ms on the next two - and the file work inside that step is logged separately and finished 11.1 seconds before the step did. What is left after it is one thing: `CaptureScrollForReloadAsync`, a JS interop call that reads the code viewer's scroll offsets so the re-render can put the user back where they were. **Remembering the scroll position is a courtesy; making someone wait eleven seconds for it is not**, so the call is now bounded at 250ms and the edit completes without it when the browser does not answer, logging how long it took whenever it exceeds 50ms. Why the first call after an idle period is slow at all is not established - a WebView that has been idle for nine minutes is the obvious suspect, and every fast case followed another click within seconds - but it no longer costs the user anything, and the log now says when it happens. The synchronous `CheckModelVcsStatus()` removed earlier was redundant and is still worth having gone; it was not the cause. |
| B254 ✅ | **The variable on the left of an assignment is coloured as a function call** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-20 from reading `Modelica.Mechanics.MultiBody.Frames.Internal.maxWithoutEvent_dd`, whose whole algorithm section is `y_dd := if u1 > u2 then u1_dd else u2_dd;` — the target came out in the function colour instead of the variable one. The grammar is `statement : component_reference (':=' expression \| function_call_args)`, so the reference is the thing being **called** in one form and the thing being **assigned to** in the other, and both were being marked as a call. **The equation path beside it had always asked the question** (`equation.function_call_args() is not null`); the statement path answered `true` unconditionally. One line in each of the two visitors: `_isFunction` scoped around the reference in `ModelicaRenderer.VisitStatement`, and `IsCallTarget`'s `StatementContext` arm in `ModelicaTokenClassifier` - which had mirrored the renderer **on purpose**, its own comment naming this case as a known difference. **Fixed on both sides** for B232's reason: the renderer is no longer what a user sees, but it is the independent implementation the classifier's 99.998% agreement is measured against, and a baseline with a known defect in it has to be explained every time it is used. Six tests, three of them controls - a call statement with no `:=`, a `(a, b) := f(x)` multiple-output call, and an equation target - and verified by reverting both visitors and watching exactly the three non-control assertions fail. Nothing in 2,023 existing parser tests had pinned the old behaviour, and the whole-library round trip (B234's opt-in test, 8,367 files) still passes. |
| B234 ✅ | **The strongest test MLQT has does not run unless someone remembers a variable** | Testing | ⭐⭐ | S | `ModelicaTokenClassifierTests.RoundTripsOverAWholeLibrary` asserts the whole fidelity claim — strip the tags and the output is the source, over **8,367 files and 1.1M lines** of real Modelica. It is opt-in through `MLQT_FIDELITY_CORPUS` because a library that size is not in the repository, which means **neither CI nor `run-mutation.ps1` ever executes it**, and that is visible in the mutation report: the emit loop's offset arithmetic is where the surviving mutants are, because the only test that would kill them does not run. The committed corpus was widened (no trailing semicolon, trailing blank lines, CR-only endings) but that is a mitigation, not a fix. Three ways out and they are not equivalent: commit a corpus large enough to exercise the emit loop (how large is the question — the shapes that matter are multi-line tokens, skipped characters and text `PreprocessCode` has to tidy, not volume); make the CI job fetch a library; or accept it as a manual gate and put it in the release checklist so it is run deliberately rather than remembered. **Run it by hand before any release that touches the classifier** — the command is in CLAUDE.md. **✅ Done 2026-09-21, and the premise was wrong.** The survivors were not alive because the corpus test does not run - **they would have survived it running**. `RoundTripsOverAWholeLibrary` calls `Highlight(string)`, which ends in `catch { return Plain(source); }`, and `Plain` returns the source as untagged lines - which round-trips perfectly. So a fault that makes the emit loop *throw* is caught, falls back, and every round-trip assertion passes, over 8,367 files exactly as over eight. **A test that only strips the tags off cannot tell `Highlight` from `Plain`.** All three options above were aimed at the wrong thing. `TheEmitLoopIsFaithfulAndStillColours` goes through the three-argument `Highlight`, which has no catch, over the five shapes that reach the guards - no trailing semicolon, trailing blank lines, trailing spaces, and a character the lexer rejects both mid-file and at the end - and asserts the output is **tagged** as well as faithful, with a control test asserting that `Plain` round-trips and carries no tags so the second assertion cannot be dropped as redundant. Measured: **22 survivors to 19, 84.72% to 86.81%**. What remains in the emit loop is read and recorded at the test rather than scored: the `StringBuilder` capacity, and two pairs that are equivalent - the loop bound and the EOF `break`, each unreachable because of the other, and two comparisons whose mutated form appends a zero-length span. One logical mutant at the guard is not equivalent and no input was found for it. **The opt-in test is unchanged and now has a home**: `Design/release-checklist.md` exists (it did not), and running the corpus is item 2 with what it does *not* cover written beside it. |
| B233 ✅ | **Hide-annotations leaves the ones written on a line with code** | Code Review | ⭐ | M | `ElisionFinder` removes a construct **as a unit or not at all**, so an annotation whose first or last line carries real code stays visible — dropping whole lines from it would leave the user looking at `Real x "d" annotation (Placement(`, which is worse than showing it. That is the right default and it hides what matters (41–44% of lines sit wholly inside an annotation, and the class-level graphics ones are written on their own lines), but the inline `Placement` annotations on declarations are exactly the noise a user asked to hide and they remain. Doing it properly means splicing **markup**, not source — the elision is applied to highlighted lines, so cutting a line at a character offset can cut inside a tag. The shape is probably for the classifier to expose where each token's markup begins so a line can be rebuilt from whole tags. Worth measuring what fraction of annotations this actually leaves before deciding it is worth that. **Confirmed from real use 2026-09-20, and it is the common case rather than an edge one**: a `connect(...)` equation almost always carries its `annotation(Line(...))` on the same line, so a user who turns annotations off sees no change at all in the equation section - which is where most of the noise is. The measurement to make first is therefore not "what fraction of annotations" but "what fraction of *equation* lines". **Also asked at the same time: why is a hidden annotation replaced by a `// annotation` marker rather than simply removed?** The marker exists so the reader can see that something is hidden rather than silently reading a class that is missing parts - but it costs a line per annotation and the user found it noisier than the thing it hides. Worth settling deliberately: no marker, a marker only where a whole line went, or a gutter mark. **✅ Done 2026-09-20, measurement first as the row asked.** Over **8,367 files** of real Modelica: **31.6% of non-blank equation-section lines carry an annotation, and 62% of those were left visible** - so a user who turned annotations off saw no change at all in the section holding most of the noise. 65.9% of all annotations were hidden; it is now 100%, and 0 of the equation lines keep one. **The implementation is not the one the row predicted.** Splicing *markup* needs the classifier to expose where each token's markup begins, and cutting at a character offset can cut inside a tag - but removing an annotation from Modelica leaves Modelica, so `ElisionFinder.WithoutAnnotations` cuts the **text** and the caller simply colours what comes back. One more parse when annotations are hidden, and no second representation of where a token starts. Line numbers are preserved, so the existing map still points a finding at the right line: an annotation running across lines is joined onto its first and the rest come back as lines to drop. **The corpus found a defect no small test would have.** Those continuation lines were reported as elided but left in the returned text, so a caller that parsed it got the orphaned tail of an annotation whose head had gone - **3,199 of 8,367 files stopped parsing**, which in the viewer means silently dropping from parse-tree colouring to lexer-only. Emptying them fixes it; the check that found it is now part of the measurement and a unit test, verified by putting the bug back. **The marker question was settled: no marker anywhere.** It cost a line per annotation and was noisier than what it hid; the toolbar button being filled is the signal. A hidden nested class still leaves one and that is a different decision - it carries the class's *name*, so a package still reads as a list of what it contains. |
| B217 ✅ | **The class diff compares stored text against the file** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | `LoadModelDiffAsync` feeds the diff `Definition.ModelicaCode` as the working copy and a slice of the **file** at HEAD. For a package whose inline children have been trimmed, or a class re-rendered by the formatter since it was read, those are not comparable documents — the trimmed children should be appearing as deletions. **Predicted, not observed**: confirm it against a package with standalone children and an uncommitted change before writing anything.
