B215's source rule fixes it as a side effect. **Confirmed, and the confirmation is the test**: a package the trimmer has touched holds text with its inline children removed, so every one of them showed as a deletion nobody made. `CodeReview.WorkingCopyText` now takes the working-copy side from `ClassSource` like the viewer does, so both sides of the diff come from the file. The control matters as much as the fix — an ordinary class, which is the large majority, still answers from its stored text without going near the filesystem. |

### Library browser and navigation

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B188 ✅ | **Repositories cannot be reordered within a project** | Library browser | ⭐⭐ | S | They appear in the order they were added, which is rarely the order of interest. The user wants the most relevant at the top. Needs a persisted order in the project settings and a way to change it. **Done, and it needed no sort key.** The list order already *is* the order — the browser renders `IRepositoryService.Repositories` straight through and `SaveRepositorySettingsAsync` writes the active project's entries from the same list, in order, which `LoadRepositorySettingsAsync` then walks sequentially. So the whole feature is `MoveRepository(id, delta)` plus up/down arrows in the Manage Repositories table. Two things worth knowing: the move is **saved where it is made**, because that tab has no Save button (the Settings page hides its action buttons for it), and a refused move saves nothing — a settings write rewrites every repository's `.mlqt/settings.json`, which is not a thing to do because an arrow was clicked at the end of the list. Out of range is a refusal rather than a clamp, so "moved" and "did nothing" stay distinguishable. |
| B189 ✅ | **Opening a model from a finding does not reveal it in the tree** | Library browser | ⭐⭐ | S | Clicking a finding opens the model in the viewer but leaves the browser wherever it was, so there is no context for what was just opened. Expand to and select the model, the way a "reveal in tree" action would. **Done**, and it reuses the tree's existing restore-expansion walk, which already knows to fetch children for a node marked expanded — a node expanded without them renders open and empty. Two things worth knowing: the chain of packages is walked through `ParentModelName` rather than by splitting the dotted name, because containment is what the tree nests by and a quoted identifier carries dots of its own (`ModelicaReference` has one); and a browser whose tree does not start at that chain's root returns without touching anything, or every repository's tree would expand for every class. A selection the tree itself made is not revealed — the user has just clicked it, and re-expanding underneath them is not help. |
| B191 ✅ | **Nothing in the tree marks a model as modified** | Library browser | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Add a marker for models with uncommitted changes, and **distinguish the kind of change**: one marker for edits that affect simulation, another for purely graphical or documentation edits. Then a filter view showing only modified models, filterable by that distinction. The classification is the substantial part — it needs a comparison of the parsed old and new class, not a text diff. **Done, and the comparison is the deliverable.** `ModelicaParser/Comparison/` reduces each class in a file to two strings — a canonical token stream with comments, descriptions and display-only annotations removed, and its own source verbatim — and compares them against the committed version class by class. Equal meaning and equal text is `Unchanged`, equal meaning and different text is `Cosmetic`, different meaning is `AffectsSimulation`. **Both strings exclude the class's nested classes**, which carry their own answers, which is what stops a one-class edit marking every package above it. **Annotations are filtered, not ignored**: `SimulationAnnotations` is a list of the display-only ones, and everything else — including every vendor annotation MLQT has not heard of — is significant, because calling a graphical edit significant costs a second look and the reverse hides a real change from someone relying on the marker. Three things this had to get right that are not obvious: a class body's `annotation (...) ;` is a statement, so the semicolon goes with it or a class that gained nothing but an icon differs by a stray `;`; an external clause's semicolon is *not* that semicolon; and the elements of an annotation are a set, so a tool that rewrites them in another order has changed nothing. The tree marks **M** (orange), **C** (blue) or nothing, every chip now has a tooltip, the descendant dot is coloured by the strongest change under it, and a row of chips above the tree narrows to Changed / Simulation / Cosmetic, each carrying its count. **The tree stays a tree** — it is pruned to the matching classes and the packages that contain them, built whole with the lazy loader withheld while a filter is on, and open exactly as far as the user had the full tree open. Three things were changed the same day after using it: the control was a dropdown over a flat list, `DescendantKinds` climbed the dotted id rather than `AncestorChain`, which is the mistake B189 recorded once already — a quoted Modelica identifier carries dots of its own — and the pruned tree first shipped fully expanded, which left the full tree spread out once the filter was cleared, the two sharing one expansion record. **When either version will not parse the answer is `Unknown`**, which reads exactly as MLQT did before this item. **Reported and fixed the same day**: none of this appears for a **reference-only** repository. MLQT never formats, checks, commits or writes to one, so there is nothing for a marker to be about — and it is not file-monitored either, so anything shown would only be refreshed by loading the project, which makes it a stale marker rather than a useful one. The whole pass is skipped, which also takes the working-copy query and the per-file committed-version reads off the startup path for every vendor checkout in a project. Same shape as B200. |
| B197 ✅ | **No way to peek at or navigate into a used class** | Library browser / Code Review | ⭐⭐ | M | Reviewing documentation, what looks like a variable name is highlighted and checking what it should be called means finding the base class by hand. Wanted: a quick peek, or navigation into the base class with a way back — ideally back through the last few classes visited, like an editor's navigation stack. **Navigation in and the way back, not the peek.** A menu on the Code Review toolbar lists the classes this one uses and opens one; back and forward arrows sit beside the class name, because every tab moves the selection and the history is one history — so it lives on `AppState` with the selection it tracks, not on a page. Going somewhere new after going back discards what was ahead, re-selecting the class already shown is not a visit (a reload re-raises the selection and would otherwise fill the history with one class), and fifty is as far back as it remembers. **What is not done is the peek**: hovering an identifier in the documentation and seeing its class without leaving. That needs a token to be resolved to a class, which the classifier's `TYPE` and `NAME` tags make possible but do not do. **And the menu is empty until dependency analysis has run**, because the edges are what it reads — it says so rather than looking like a class that uses nothing. |
| B173 ✅ | **The resource tree has no horizontal scrollbar and cannot be resized** | External resources | ⭐⭐ | S | A long path in the External Resources tree is simply cut off: the panel neither scrolls sideways nor resizes. Either would do; both is better, and a splitter here would be the same control B186 wants on the Code Review page. **Both done**: the fixed 4/8 grid became the same `MudExSplitPanel`, and the pane scrolls on both axes (`overflow: auto`, not `overflow-y`) so a path too long for whatever width the user chose can still be read. |
| B199 ✅ | **The dependency plot is unusable above a few hundred nodes** | Dependencies | ⭐⭐ | S | Over 1,000 nodes the Cytoscape graph is very slow to generate and the nodes are too small to read, so it costs a lot and shows nothing. Above a threshold, skip the plot and show the list alone — with a way to draw it anyway if the user insists. **✅ Done 2026-09-20, threshold measured rather than guessed.** Timed in a real browser, a layout settles in 377ms at 250 nodes, 435ms at 500, 539ms at 1,000 and **2,149ms at 2,000** for a force-directed layout - so generation only bites at the top end, and **that is not what makes the picture useless**. In a 400px panel five hundred nodes are a couple of pixels each. The limit is therefore about legibility: **500**, chosen with the numbers on the table, above which the impact list is shown alone with a **Draw it anyway** button. The override is per selection, so one decision to draw a large graph does not quietly disable the limit for the session. **The test is weaker than it should be and the gap is recorded rather than papered over**: the decision is a predicate with ten tests, but the one line of markup that calls it is uncovered. A rendered test was written and abandoned - the page runs its analysis twice during initialisation, asynchronously, so an assertion against the markup is a race. Worth returning to if the page is ever made testable; writing it down beats pretending the wiring is held. |

### Projects, repositories and startup

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B198 ✅ | **A newly added repository did not load its library until MLQT was restarted, and has not reproduced** | Repositories | ⭐⭐ | M | Reported once, against `ModelicaEditorTestsGit`; restarting the tool loaded it. **Not reproduced after several attempts** on a build carrying the fixes below. Two candidates were investigated and both are ruled out. The *layout* — a single library with `package.mo` at the top level — is disproven by `AddRepositoryLoadsItsLibraryTests`: such a library is discovered, loaded, recorded on the repository, announced to the tree, and visible to the `Libraries.Where(RepositoryId == id)` query MainLayout gates its analysis on, with a subdirectory layout as the control. **B204** — the discovered library being labelled with the folder name rather than its own — would have looked like this, but the library in that repository has the same name as its folder, so it was never visible there. **Left open at low confidence**, like B166. It is worth noting the report came before B192 was fixed, and that fix removed a path on which a *different* project's repositories were loaded at startup and left loaded — enough to confuse what belonged to what. If it recurs, B206 now makes it report itself: the two silent outcomes (nothing found, nothing loadable) each say so, so the next sighting can distinguish "no library discovered" from "discovered but not loaded" from "loaded but not shown", which the original report could not. **Fixed 2026-09-27, reproduced at last**: the library was always loaded, owned, in the tree data and analysed; what stayed empty was the repository's own browser. `LoadLibrariesAsync` let each library announce itself to the tree (from `AddLibraryFromPathAsync`) before it set the library's `RepositoryId` and added it to `Repository.LibraryIds`, and `LibraryBrowser` filters its tree by exactly that membership - so a browser that rebuilt in between showed the repository empty, and nothing told it again until a restart. A harness rendering a real `LibraryBrowser` per repository, as MainLayout does, over fresh Git repositories with `package.mo` at the top: 18 of 150 adds came up empty (fresh 5/30, after a project switch 8/30, beside another project's repositories 5/30), with every announcement ahead of the membership. `LoadLibrariesAsync` now loads under one `SuppressTreeDataChanged` scope, as `SwitchProjectAsync` and `RefreshRepositoryAsync` already did, so the one announcement comes after membership is set: 0 of 350 adds empty afterwards. Held by `AddRepositoryLoadsItsLibraryTests.TheTreeIsToldOnlyOnceTheLibraryIsRecordedOnItsRepository`. |
| B190 ✅ | **The UI, and sometimes the whole desktop, freezes during loading** | Performance | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Reported during the loading step: MLQT's window is unresponsive, and at some points other windows are slow to redraw too — moving the MLQT window is sluggish and everything can stall for a few seconds. **Confirm it still happens before investigating**; it predates several fixes since, and the log records phase durations that would say where the time goes. Whatever is blocking is on a thread it should not be on. **✅ Confirmed 2026-09-20 by the user running it, and the premise is retired: it does not freeze.** Neither MLQT nor the desktop. What is left is narrower and exactly diagnosable - *“during style checking when you click to drag the window it doesn't move with the mouse but it does jump to the new position when you release”*, plus stuttering rather than smooth dragging while loading. On Windows that is the signature of a **message pump that is not running**, and the desktop host runs its pump on the same thread Blazor renders on. **Measured**: a style check delivers findings a class at a time - 5,250 findings in **1,397 batches** for MSL on five rules, median batch **two** - and every batch raised `OnLogMessagesChanged`. Each raise cost that thread a **copy of the whole findings list** (the `LogMessages` property copies), a **sort of it** (`OrderedFindings` re-sorts on every render), a **full scan** for misspellings, and a **full re-render** of the page. So the work is O(batches × findings) on the one thread that must stay free. Fixed by coalescing: the first change is announced at once, anything inside 250ms collapses into one trailing announcement, and clearing stays immediate because the user is watching for it. 1,397 batches now produce fewer than 20 notifications. **And one of the costs was mine, from earlier the same day**: B247 added `all.Count(FilterFunc1)` to the findings heading - a full pass with a string split per element, on every render - which is now taken only when a filter is actually set. Four tests, the important one being that **the last change is always announced**: a throttle that swallows the trailing edge trades a stutter for a table that is quietly wrong. **Second round, and this time it really did freeze.** The user reported the window ignoring clicks to focus or move it for a long time during a Claytex check - a *blocked* pump, not a busy one - and the log gives the number: `Background style checking completed for (Claytex)` at **17:20:28**, `All background style checking completed` at **17:21:40**. **72 seconds after the workers had finished**, with nothing logged in between. That is the dispatcher draining a queue. `MainLayout.OnStyleFindingsFound` marshalled **every batch** through `InvokeAsync` - one queued work item per class with findings, 21,673 classes checked - onto the one thread that has to stay free. **It never needed to**: `CodeReviewService` locks around its list, so the store was always safe to call from a worker. The hop is gone; what does need the dispatcher - the render - is raised by the store, coalesced, and marshalled by whichever component is listening. `RecomputeMisspelledWords` and `RecountFindings` moved inside their `InvokeAsync` because they write component state and now run on a worker's thread. **One risk the change introduces and one flake that hinted at it**: a notification can now outrun a tab switch, so a component may be disposed between the event and its `InvokeAsync`; `TabNavigationJourney` failed once on exactly that shape and passed on every rerun, and both subscribers now swallow `ObjectDisposedException` for it. **Also visible in the same log**: with B174 in, Claytex's `MissingUnits` fell 2,076s → 334s and **`coverage` is now 69% of the check at 133ms/class** - the next thing worth measuring. |

### Analysis correctness

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B184 ✅ | **`--changed-from` re-checks everything it loaded** | CLI / performance | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | A changed-file check still runs every rule over every model, then filters. It should apply the rules only to the models in the modified files and compare those against the **baseline records for those models**. Everything still has to be *loaded* — base classes and reference resolution need it — but it need not be re-checked. The one analysis that genuinely has to run over everything is reference validation, and only when a model was deleted or renamed. This is the largest available win on check time in CI. **✅ Done 2026-09-20. Measured on the Modelica Standard Library**: a full check is 12.46s over 6,487 models, and `--changed-from HEAD~1` is **4.70s over 19** - **62% off** a typical CI run, with the remainder being the load, which cannot be skipped. **The sequencing moved as the plan said it must**: deciding which models a change touched needs the model→file map, which needs the load, so `CheckPipeline` takes a *callback* - given the map, return the models to check - rather than a parameter nobody could supply in time. `ClassLocation.ForGraph` moved ahead of the check to feed it; it only ever needed the graph. **The decision the plan demanded first, made and written into `cli.md` before any code moved**: a run asking for `--metrics`, `--min-coverage` or `--coverage-ratchet` **checks everything**, because coverage over the dozen models a commit touched is not that library's coverage and a ratchet recording it would move the baseline to a figure nothing can be compared against. It says so rather than narrowing quietly. **Both traps handled**: `StaleEntries` was already filtered to changed models on the output side and those models *were* checked, so it stays correct; `ModelsChecked` now counts what was checked rather than what was loaded, or a narrowed run would report the whole library's count and be indistinguishable from a full one. Five tests, verified by making the pipeline ignore the selection - two fail, including the one that matters most: an **unchanged model with the same finding must not be reported**. |


### Rules and formatting

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B175 ✅ | **Exclude-from-formatting writes a name into the settings file rather than an annotation** | Formatting | ⭐⭐ | S | The button records the class in `FormattingExcludedModels`, which is name-based and so does not survive a rename. `__MLQT(format=false)` is the rename-safe mechanism the documentation already recommends, both mechanisms are already honoured everywhere (B39, B65), and the writer already exists for suppression. The button should write the annotation. **Done 2026-09-19.** The button now writes `__MLQT(format=false)` into the class, through `MlqtSuppressionWriter`, and reads its own state through `FormattingExclusion.Excludes` rather than the name list alone - it was showing a class carrying the annotation as *not* excluded, so the button offered to exclude something that already was. **The row said the writer already existed, and half of it did.** Adding needed a bare directive rather than an entry appended to a quoted list, and *removing* one had never been needed by anything: the toggle has two directions, so `TryRemoveFormattingOptOutFromFile` is new. Removal widens as each container empties - the argument while `__MLQT` still holds something else, the whole `__MLQT(...)` when it does not, and the whole annotation and its `;` when that was all it held - because the alternative is leaving `annotation(__MLQT());` in a user's file. Re-including also clears the name list, or the button could not undo what earlier versions of itself had done. Reverting a modified file now happens *before* the annotation is written rather than after, which would have discarded it. 21 tests, including add/remove round trips that must return the original file byte for byte, and one across the two assemblies asserting that what the writer writes is what `FormattingExclusion` reads. |
| B177 ✅ | **No warning when a package is stored as one file instead of a directory** | Rules | ⭐⭐ | M | A package held entirely in a single `.mo` file rather than as a directory of standalone classes is a structural choice worth flagging in some repositories and not others, so it needs a rule id, a severity, a switch in the settings dialog and a row in the Findings list — not a hard-coded warning. Belongs with `PackageOrderAnalyzer`'s family. **Done 2026-09-19.** `MLQT.Structure.SingleFilePackage`, a graph analysis beside `PackageOrderAnalyzer`. The judgement is **could this be split**, not how big the file is: a child that cannot be stored standalone - `replaceable`, `redeclare`, `inner`, `outer` - has to live in its parent's file, so a package made only of those is correctly one file and is not reported, and neither is a package someone has already begun splitting, because "entirely in one file" is the claim. Off by default, like every rule, which is the point of it being a rule: single-file storage is a repository's choice. Nine tests, and the Structure category is catalog-driven so the settings dialog picked it up with no layout row. |
| B181 ✅ | **"Components before classes" can only be enforced by the formatter, never reported** | Rules | ⭐⭐ | S | The composition ordering rule is formatting-only: it is applied when the renderer rewrites a class and there is no way to be *told* about it. If the rule is switched on it should raise a finding like every other ordering rule. Note this row is the one in `settings-reference.md` with no rule id to bind its label to (B103), so giving it an id closes that gap as well. **Done 2026-09-19.** `MLQT.Style.ComponentsBeforeClasses`, with the visitor, the catalogue row, the layout row, the settings-reference id (which closes B103's gap - that row was the only one with no id to bind its label to) and the MCP toggle. Two things were not obvious from the row. **The section is the unit, not the class**: the renderer groups components before classes within each `public`/`protected` section and never moves an element across the boundary, so a class definition in one section followed by a component in the next is correctly ordered and comparing over the whole class would report the arrangement formatting produces. And its prerequisite is `ImportStatementsFirst`, not `OneOfEachSection` like its neighbours, because the renderer reads the option only inside the branch imports-first selects; the chain resolves transitively, so naming the nearer one states the real dependency. The settings switch now binds through `IsRuleSwitchedOn` like the four beside it, which it could not while it was a plain bool. |
| B236 ✅ | **Formatting strips the trailing blank line from every file, so a reformat rewrites the whole library** | Rules / formatting | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-19 from ordinary use. **Format All Files** now writes every file without a final newline where it used to end with one, so a user who reformats sees *every file in the library* as modified against a revision formatted by an earlier build — a diff of thousands of files in which nothing has actually changed, and which buries any real change in it. **A regression, and it predates 2026.4.0**: the reported comparison is against a library formatted by an earlier version, and the release reproduces it. Two things worth knowing before hunting it. The write path ends `var code = string.Join("\n", visitor.Code)` (`ModelicaPackageSaver.cs:191` and `:357`) handed to `ModelicaFileEncoding.WriteAllText`, which writes exactly what it is given — so a trailing newline can only come from `visitor.Code` ending in an empty line, and the question is what used to put it there. And `ModelicaParserHelper.PreprocessCode` does `NormalizeLineEndings(...).TrimEnd()` before the parse, which is one way a trailing blank line stops existing early. **Bisect rather than reason**: this is a one-character output difference over a path several rewrites have crossed, and `git log` on those two files against a formatted fixture will find it faster than reading will. Whatever the cause, the fix wants a test that writes a file and asserts the last character, because nothing currently looks. **Fixed 2026-09-19, and the diagnosis in this row was wrong: it is not a regression.** `git log -S` settles it - the incremental formatter has appended a trailing newline since the initial public commit and the full library save has joined the rendered lines without one since the same commit. The two have never agreed, so which path last touched a file decided how it ended, and the user's own guess on the phase note - that the earlier reformat went a different route - was right. `package.order` was already terminated, because `WriteAllLines` mirrors `File.WriteAllLines`, so one save left the two kinds of file in a package ending differently. The rule now lives in `ModelicaFileEncoding.EnsureFinalNewline`, applied by every write and public so a caller comparing what is on disk against what it would write compares like with like - MCP's `format_class` needed exactly that, or it would have reported every formatted file as changed and rewritten it on each call. **Measured over MSL**: 5,753 files, none left without a final newline, and of the 2,480 files both paths rewrote the two now produce identical bytes for 2,471. The 9 that differ are all `package.mo` and are not a formatting disagreement - the full save extracts inline standalone children into their own files and the incremental path does not, which is the documented one-file-per-class restructure. Six tests, three of which state the route-independence property rather than the newline. |
| B195 ✅ | **`package.order` completeness, with an option to align with Dymola** | Rules | ⭐⭐ | M | Dymola emits, of its own accord on load: *"Warning: The package.order is incomplete, since the class Bad in file/directory .../Sub/Bad.mo is missing."* That is exactly the "a warning may be given" the specification anticipates, and it confirms the shape recommended for MLQT's own check. **It fires only for the `package.order` shape** — Dymola never descends into non-package folders, so it gives no signal at all for the stray-file case, and that half of MLQT's check has no upstream equivalent and earns its keep on its own. Worth an explicit "match Dymola" setting so a repository can choose to report only what Dymola would. **Done 2026-09-19, and the row was wrong about the interesting half.** `PackageOrderMatchesDymola` narrows `MLQT.Structure.PackageOrder` to what Dymola's loader would warn about. It is a modifier on one rule rather than a rule of its own - it decides which findings are reported, not whether anything is checked - so it is a switch under the analysis rules rather than a severity picker among them. **The 'stray-file case' this row rests on does not exist**: MLQT does not descend into a directory without a `package.mo` either (`LibraryDataService`, same reason Dymola does not), so there is no half of the check with no upstream equivalent on that side. Found by running the CLI over a fixture with a file in a plain folder and watching it not load. What the setting really drops is two things: **stale entries**, which Dymola says nothing about, and **a class in a file whose name does not match it** - MLQT reads every `.mo` in a package directory and takes each class's own name, so it loads `model Odd` out of `Widget.mo` and reports it unlisted, while Dymola resolves by file name and cannot load it from there at all. That second one is worth keeping in the default answer: a class the tool of record cannot find is a worse problem than the one this rule usually reports. Eight tests plus an end-to-end CLI check of both answers. |
| B174 ✅ | **Loading and style checking are getting slow** | Performance | ⭐⭐⭐ | L | Reported from ordinary use. B128 established the series and the per-phase breakdown — `CheckTimings` is in place and `mlqt check --timings` prints it — and concluded the growth to ~300 s was expected rather than a regression, with no next obvious win at the time. **Start from the log**, which records timestamped phase durations going back weeks, and from `--timings` on the current build; do not theorise first. B184 and B147 are the two named candidates. **Measured 2026-09-20, before touching anything, which is what this package's gate asks for - and the answer is not where the plan assumed.** From the application log, per class of real library: MSL 27.7ms total (parse 4.2ms, coverage 6.2ms), Claytex **314.4ms** (parse 11.3ms, coverage 161.4ms, MissingUnits 95.8ms). Checking MSL *inside* the Claytex session cost 24.7ms/class, unchanged - so it is **not the size of the graph**, which was the standing suspicion. Reproduced locally with the CLI on libraries in the repository's reach: with the same five rules on, `rule:MissingUnits` is **82% of the whole check on Buildings and 94% on MSL**, at 63.6 and 65.5 ms/class - within 3% of each other, so it is not a property of one library. **Parse is 6-17%**, which retires the assumption that B235 explains a useful share of this: it does not. **One change made, and two hypotheses tested and rejected first**, which is the part worth keeping. `CreateUnitLookup` memoised the *answer* - `unitCache` is keyed by the id of the class a type resolved to, so it holds nothing at all for a type that does not resolve, and resolving is the expensive half. Memoising the *question* `(class, type)` took MissingUnits from 425.1s to 295.4s on MSL and 477.8s to 310.6s on Buildings, with all 5,250 and 3,285 findings identical. Rejected: caching `TypeResolver.CollectAncestors` per class (**no measurable change at all**), and the theory that resolving a short name through an extends chain is dearer than resolving a qualified one (a synthetic library measured it the *other way round*, 99ms against 268ms). **~45ms/class remains unexplained** and wants a profiler rather than more reading - two guesses have already been wrong, which is the B253 lesson arriving again in the same package it was learned in. **Profiled 2026-09-20 with `dotnet-trace`, and the mechanism is now known.** Of real CPU (idle excluded), ~43% is ANTLR ATN prediction - the run is *parsing*, not resolving - and 76% of all non-idle samples have `MissingUnits` on the stack. The path between them, printed frame by frame: `MissingUnits.VisitComponent_declaration -> TypeResolver.ResolveWithInheritance -> CollectAncestors -> Walk -> EnsureParsed/ParseWithErrors`, **82% of the rule's time**. Collecting a class's extends chain reads each ancestor's interface, which parses it when nobody is holding its tree, and nothing memoised the chain - so a class with twenty unresolved type names re-parsed its whole ancestry twenty times. `TypeResolver.AncestorCache` now holds it for the run, scoped to the lookup rather than the graph because the graph is reloaded after a format or a VCS operation. **Measured end to end**: `MissingUnits` 425.1s -> 128.7s of thread-time on MSL and 477.8s -> 132.1s on Buildings, whole check 450.9s -> 159.5s and 579.4s -> 238.1s, with all 5,250 and 3,285 findings identical. **Parse is now the largest remaining phase on Buildings at 43%**, which is where B235 becomes worth taking. **The hour this cost is the lesson**: the ancestor cache was tried early, measured as "no change at all", and rejected - because the binary under test had not been rebuilt. The same change measured properly was a 56% cut. **Check the change is in the assembly before believing a negative result**; the profiling recipe is now in CLAUDE.md so the next person starts from a stack and not from a guess. **✅ Done 2026-09-20**, with WP4 - the reported symptom is fixed and the mechanism is known. The row was left without its tick while the backlog and the phase note disagreed about it, which is how an item that is finished goes on reading as outstanding work. **What is not finished is a different cause and now has its own id**: coverage measurement, measured at 69% of a check once the unit-resolution work was in, is B261. |
| B261 ✅ | **Coverage measurement is the largest single cost left in a check** | Performance | ⭐⭐ | M | With B174's unit-resolution work in, `coverage` is **69% of a check at 133 ms/class** - larger than any single rule and larger than parse. **The figure is recorded here without the library and build it was taken on, so re-measure with `mlqt check --timings` before touching anything**, which is what this phase's performance gate asks for in any case. **It is the same family as B174, and the first hypothesis is already visible in the code**: `UnitResolver.Resolve` takes an optional `TypeResolver.AncestorCache`, and `CoverageMeasurer` (`MetricsCalculator.cs:321` and `:336`) passes none - so measuring a class walks and re-parses its ancestry exactly the way `MissingUnits` did before that cache existed, which was worth 3x there. Its `_unitCache` is keyed by the **resolved class's id**, which is the *answer* shape B174 found insufficient; keying the *question* `(class, type)` was worth 30% on its own. **Neither is a reason to skip the profiler** - two guesses were wrong in B174 and the stack is what settled it. While there, ask whether `CoverageDimensions.ForClass` is doing per-class work that belongs per repository. **Done 2026-09-24 (WP13). The figure was Claytex**, and it reproduced with the CLI before anything was touched: the whole `ModelicaLibrariesTrunk/Modelica` repository against the Dymola 2026x Refresh 1 library folder, 21,673 classes, coverage **2,901.9s (66%)** of 4,374.5s thread-time against `rule:MissingUnits` **389.1s** — the rule and the dimension asking the same question about the same components, and the dimension paying 7.5 times as much. That was the mechanism, and the hypothesis above was right: `CoverageMeasurer` called `UnitResolver` with a cache keyed by the resolved class and no ancestor cache, so it had neither of the caches B174 gave the rule. It now takes a unit lookup, and `StyleCheckContext` builds **one** — `StyleChecking.CreateUnitLookup`, both caches — and gives it to the rule and the measurer both, so each `(class, type)` question is resolved once between them; a measurer with nothing to share (the Metrics tab) builds its own with the same caches. **Measured on the same repository, build and settings: coverage 2,901.9s → 323.6s, total thread-time 4,374.5s → 1,542.6s, wall clock 6m51s → 4m21s**, with all 104,643 findings identical and every coverage figure identical (the metrics snapshots differ only in their timestamps). The profiler was not needed: the rule's own number, in the same table, was the control. `CoverageDimensions.ForClass` **was then measured and is not the cost**: 0.34µs a call over Claytex's 15,760 reported class ids and its own settings, **7.4ms for a whole check**, and `TrackedFor` — the repository-wide half — is 4.4µs and already asked once per repository by every caller. What it does per class is one excluded-library match against three compiled patterns, an early return, and a search of a three-entry list. **Then profiled with `dotnet-trace`**, since coverage was still ~15ms/class: 59% of what remained was `OneOfEachSection.VisitComposition` run by the layout measurement, and **`RuleContext.GetText` was 30% of all non-idle time in the whole check**. Four loops over a class body's children asked every child for its text to find the `public`/`protected`/`external` keywords — which for an element list or an equation section rebuilds its whole source, and an outer class's source includes its nested classes, each walked again. `SectionKeyword.Of` reads keywords from terminals only; `OneOfEachSection`, `PublicParametersAndConstantsHaveDescription`, `FollowNamingConvention` and `ModelicaRenderer` all use it, and `OneOfEachSection`'s "is there an implicit public section" asks whether the list holds any token rather than how long its text is (a comment alone still counts, as it did — pinned, and verified by mutation). Same repository: thread-time **1,542.6s → 1,088.7s**, coverage 323.6s → 161.2s, and those three rules (193s, 109s, 108s) now below the top ten; findings and coverage identical again. **The wall clock moved only 4m21s → 4m12s, and the trace says why**: the per-class phase runs across 24 cores and is 68s of a 257s run, while dependency analysis (74s) and the whole-graph analyses (72s, one after another on one thread — `UnusedMembersAnalyzer` 41s, `ShadowingAnalyzer` 23s, `UnusedImportAnalyzer` 9s) are serial. That is where the next wall-clock gain is, and it is not this item's. |

### External tools

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B170 ✅ | **An OpenModelica check reports nothing at all** | External tools | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Checking a class with OpenModelica produces no visible feedback: it opens no window, and there is no dialog reporting success or failure. The Dymola equivalent is only known to have run because Dymola's own window appears. Both need a result dialog of MLQT's own, reporting what the tool said — which also removes the dependence on a vendor window being visible. **✅ Done 2026-09-20.** `OnModelChecked` only recorded a finding when a check *failed*, and `OnCheckingComplete` closed the progress dialog and said nothing - so a clean check produced **no window, no dialog and no finding**, and the only evidence one had run was that the button had been pressed. Every result is now kept, not just the failures, and a dialog at the end names the tool and says how it went in one sentence: *“Dymola checked 27 classes with no problems reported”*, *“OpenModelica reported problems with 3 of 27 classes”*, *“Dymola check stopped after 5 classes”*. Failures quote the **tool's own message** rather than paraphrasing it - a dialog saying “check failed” only sends the user to the tool to find out why, which is what running it was meant to save - and they still go to the findings table, so closing the dialog loses nothing. Eight tests on the sentence, including the two wordings that would read as bugs: **“1 of 1 class”** and a cancelled run reported as a pass. **Reported wrong on first use 2026-09-21: a successful Dymola check said “Dymola checked nothing”.** The sentence was right and its input was empty - both services fired `OnModelChecked` **only for failures**, with a comment saying so *(“to reduce UI updates”)*, so a clean run produced no results to count. That saving is not one worth having now that the subscriber coalesces its own re-renders (B190). Every result is reported. **And `checkModel` returning true does not mean the tool had nothing to say** - the user's point: a model that is fine and one that is fine apart from six warnings both return true, and `getLastError()` is where the difference is. `ModelCheckResult` gains a `Log` separate from `ErrorMessage`, because putting warnings in a field called ErrorMessage makes every one of them look like a failure; the dialog shows it for a clean check too, when there is something to show. **One thing this had to get right to be safe**: Dymola's log accumulates, so it is cleared before the check and read after - otherwise a model that checked cleanly could be shown the error from one checked before it, which is a worse answer than no log at all. OpenModelica's `getErrorString` drains as it reads, so the equivalent there is to read and discard first. **Untestable without the tool**: neither service has a seam - `DymolaInterface` and `OpenModelicaInterface` are concrete - so nothing here guards “report every result”. That is why this was found by using it twice. **Reported again on first use 2026-09-21: OpenModelica refused to open the model at all.** Both services opened **the class's own file** - for `Modelica.Blocks.Continuous.Integrator`, `Integrator.mo`. A class stored in its own file is only called that because of the packages above it, and handed that file alone OpenModelica sees a class named `Integrator` with nothing to resolve its `within` against, and refuses. Dymola accepts it and discovers the enclosing package itself, which is the whole reason the same code worked for one tool and not the other - and exactly the shape where fixing only the broken one leaves the pair disagreeing about the same question. `LibraryRootFile.For(graph, model)` is that question asked once: the first segment of the class's name, resolved through the graph to the file that node came from, falling back to the class's own file for a one-file library or a class with no package above it. **Both** services use it. Seven tests, three of which fail against the previous behaviour - including one asserting only that the answer is *not* the class's own file, because that is what the fix is. |
| B171 ✅ | **Dymola checking fails on the second attempt if its window was closed** | External tools | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | The first check works; closing the Dymola window and checking again does not. The interface presumably keeps a connection or process handle that is no longer valid and does not detect it. It should notice a dead session and start a new one — and, with B170 in place, say so if it cannot. **✅ Done 2026-09-20.** `GetOrCreateAsync` returned the cached instance **without asking whether it was still there**. Closing Dymola's window ends its process and its JSON-RPC server, and nothing told the object holding the connection - `IsOfflineMode` is a flag set at construction, not a probe. **The probe already existed**: `DymolaInterface.PingAsync`, short-lived client, two-second timeout, written for scanning candidate ports. `IsAliveAsync` now asks the two questions cheapest first - a process we started that has exited needs no network call; anything else is pinged - and the factory drops and rebuilds rather than reconnecting, because the instance owns an `HttpClient` and a process handle that both describe the session that has gone. **Five tests, and the first four were worthless**: they all stopped at the `_isOffline` guard, so stubbing the ping to `return true` passed every one. The fifth stands up an `HttpListener`, connects to it, stops it, and asks again - the reported scenario as closely as it can be had without the tool - and it kills that mutant. **What is still unverified**: the factory actually rebuilding, because `GetOrCreateAsync` *starts* Dymola when it finds no live session, so the path cannot be exercised without launching it. Verified by hand is what the package asks for, and the 219-test Dymola suite passes against the live tool on this machine. |
| B259 ✅ | **Nothing happens for several seconds after the check button is pressed** | External tools | ⭐⭐ | S | Checking one class showed nothing at all until the check finished - no dialog, no progress, no sign the button had done anything. Worse for OpenModelica, which has no window of its own to appear. **✅ Done 2026-09-21.** The progress dialog existed but was opened **only for a package**, and even then it opened onto nothing: a check does not begin with the first class - the tool has to be started and the library opened, which is most of the wait on a real library - and both counts are zero throughout that, so the dialog read *0 checked out of 0*. Three changes, each small: the dialog opens on the click whatever is being checked; `ModelCheckProgress` carries a `Status` for what is happening before there is anything to count, which both services report as they start the tool and as they open the library (*“Opening package.mo in OpenModelica…”* - worth naming, since which file that is had just changed in B170); and the title counts classes only when there is more than one, because *0 of 1 classes checked* is a progress bar with nothing to say. Four service tests assert the **order** rather than the content - the first event reaches the UI while the factory is still blocked, which is the reported complaint stated as a test - and all four fail if the status is not reported. |
| B260 ✅ | **A headless OpenModelica process is left running after MLQT closes** | External tools | ⭐⭐ | S | `omc` has no window and no owner, so one started for a model check outlives MLQT and sits there until somebody finds it in a task manager and wonders what it is. **✅ Done 2026-09-21.** The factory is now `IDisposable` and the host ends the session after `app.Run()` returns. **Dymola is deliberately not treated this way** - its window is visible, the user may have carried on working in the session MLQT started, and closing it from underneath them would lose that work; what makes the omc process worth ending is exactly what makes it invisible, and a test states that as a decision rather than leaving it to look like an oversight. Found on the way: `OpenModelicaInterface.Dispose` set `_isDisposed` **before** calling `ExitAsync`, which asks `IsConnected` - so the graceful `quit()` had never once been sent and omc was always killed, leaving its temporary directory behind. Four guards, of which two are the whole of what is testable without OpenModelica installed; deleting the host's one-line call first left them green, because `ShutDownOpenModelica(` also matches the method's own declaration below it. |
| B262 ✅ | **PR #9 reshapes the `DymolaInterface` API, and nothing in MLQT uses any of it** | External tools | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | PR #9 (`dymola-command-timeout`) removes the five-minute `HttpClient.Timeout` — which could never be raised afterwards, because the constructor’s probe sends a request and `HttpClient.Timeout` is fixed once one has gone out — and replaces it with a per-request `CancellationTokenSource`. **It grew well past that in review** and now carries four things MLQT must answer for, none of which it uses: a `CommandTimeout` property (default five minutes, at most `MaxCommandTimeout` ≈ 49.7 days, or `Timeout.InfiniteTimeSpan`); an optional per-call `timeout` **and** `CancellationToken` on the thirteen wrappers that can run long; a constructor `connectionWindow` (default 30s) that keeps asking a Dymola which accepts the connection but is too busy to answer; and `ProcessId`. **Three consequences, and the second is a defect waiting for the merge.** (1) `DymolaSettings` needs the timeout field, the factory needs to apply it, `SettingsExternalTools` needs to show it, and the field has to reject anything above `MaxCommandTimeout` — the interface throws there, and `CallDymolaFunctionAsync` swallows the throw, so an out-of-range setting would read as every command failing. (2) **The constructor can now block ~32s synchronously** while it waits out a busy Dymola, and `DymolaInterfaceFactory.GetOrCreateAsync` calls it inside its own lock — off the UI thread or the window freezes. (3) `DymolaCheckingService` already holds a `_cancellationTokenSource` that today only stops *between* models; passing it through the new parameter is what finally makes Cancel interrupt a check in flight, which is a user-visible win in its own right and was not previously available at any price. `ProcessId` remains the cheaper liveness question than the ping B171 settled on for a session MLQT started itself. **A fourth thing, found approving the PR and not asked of the contributor: `SetOfflineMode(true)` is now a no-op.** The recovery probe added in review cannot tell “offline because Dymola was unreachable” from “offline because the caller said so”, so the next command probes, finds Dymola, clears the flag and runs — measured. `StopDymolaProcessAsync` has the same shape: it sets offline, and a later command could reconnect to a *different* Dymola on that port. Dormant today because nothing in MLQT calls `SetOfflineMode`, only `IsOfflineMode` (the factory and `Examples.cs`), which is why it was not worth holding the PR for. **The suite cannot see it** — `HelperTypeTests.Offline_SetOfflineMode_MutatesFlag` asserts the flag round-trips and never that a command is suppressed, the same shape as the harness that could not see the original fix. A separate `_forcedOffline` the probe does not clear closes it. **Do it before wiring anything up**, or the first caller to want “work offline” in the UI finds the switch does nothing. **Take it with B263, which is the user-facing half of the same question.** **Done 2026-09-24 (WP14), after merging `main` into `backlog`**: PR #9 had reached `main` only, and met B229's `IDymolaInterface` here for the first time — the text merged cleanly and the types did not, so the interface took the new `timeout`/`CancellationToken` parameters in the merge commit. Then, in the order this row asks: **`_forcedOffline`**, which the probe does not clear and `StopDymolaProcessAsync` sets too, with tests against a Dymola that answers — the one the old test could not see — verified by mutation; the **constructor and `StartDymolaProcessAsync` moved onto the thread pool** in `GetOrCreateAsync`; **`DymolaSettings.CommandTimeoutMs`** (default 300,000, 0 for none; an `int` of milliseconds cannot exceed `MaxCommandTimeout`, so no stored value is one the interface refuses) applied on every hand-out; and the **token passed into the check**, so Cancel ends one in flight. One thing the row did not foresee: every command answers `false` whatever went wrong, so a timed-out check read as a failed model and the service then asked for the log — waiting out the limit again against a Dymola still busy. `CommandOutcome` / `LastOutcome` says why. `ProcessId` is still unused. |
| B263 ✅ | **A user cannot give an external tool longer for a large model** | External tools | ⭐⭐ | M | A complex model can take longer to check than the wrapper is prepared to wait, and nothing in the UI lets the user say so. **The two tools are in different states and neither is right.** PR #9 gives Dymola a `CommandTimeout` with no setting behind it (B262). `OpenModelicaSettings` has carried `CommandTimeoutMs` (60s) and `StartupTimeoutMs` (5s) since it was written and **neither is read by anything** - `SendCommandAsync` does a bare `ReceiveFrameString()` with no timeout at all, so a long omc check blocks indefinitely and `StartAsync` waits a hardcoded 2,000ms instead. Needs: omc honouring its own settings (`TryReceiveFrameString`), one timeout per tool in the External Tools settings tab, and the checking services passing it down. **A settings field nothing reads is worse than no field** - it is a knob that does not work, which is what both of these are today, and B259's progress dialog is where a long wait now has to stay honest. **Done 2026-09-24 (WP14), as one change across both tools.** omc: the receive is bounded, and so is the send — a REQ socket blocks in `SendFrame` until a peer connects, so bounding only the receive left start-up unbounded; a timeout or a cancel closes the session (the socket cannot be reused and omc is still busy), the factory replaces a closed one, and `StartAsync` waits `StartupTimeoutMs` instead of sleeping two seconds. Two defects on the way: the stdout reader spun a thread at full speed once omc exited, and **no wait may be under a millisecond** — NetMQ truncates, and a 1 ms start-up limit measured waiting out omc's whole start. Found in passing and fixed: **`CodeReview` never applied the OpenModelica settings at all**, so the path and port set in the tab never reached omc — the sibling shape this row warned of. The External Tools tab has a **Check time limit (seconds)** per tool, labelled from `ToolTimeLimit.SettingName`, the same constant the timed-out result names. Both services report a timeout as `TimedOut` ("ran out of time", not "Check Failed"), do not ask for the log after it, stop a package run there, and return no result for a cancelled check — five contract tests run against both tools, verified by mutation; six live omc tests; four settings-tab tests. |

### MCP server and McpTester

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B179 ✅ | **MCP loads an encrypted library but returns almost nothing about it** | MCP | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | An agent can load an encrypted library, but the tools do not return the public interface or the documentation — both of which **are** recovered from the vendor's help HTML and are sitting on the synthesized stub: description, base classes, parameters, connectors, inputs and outputs. `get_class_info` and its neighbours should return them for a stub, clearly marked as recovered from documentation rather than read from source, and still declaring the class not editable (B85). **Fixed 2026-09-22.** The row said those members were sitting on the stub; they were not. `DymolaHelpParser` recovered them, `ExternalStubBuilder.BuildNode` dropped them, and nothing in the repository had read a `DocumentedMember` since the day it was written - so the answer `get_class_interface` gave for a vendor class with a dozen parameters was *no parameters*. **Why they could not simply join the synthesized source**: a Modelica declaration needs a type, the generator publishes none, and `parameter Real k` is a fabrication that reaches `TypeResolver` and `UnitResolver` looking exactly like something read from source. So the `DocumentedClass` is kept whole on `ModelNode.RecoveredFromDocumentation` - the single, stated exception to "synthesize source, never metadata", taken because there is no truthful declaration to write - and **nothing that checks, resolves or writes reads it**. `get_class_interface` and `list_class_elements` return the parameters, connectors and function signature with the description and unit the vendor published, every `type` null, and all three of those tools plus `get_class_info` carry `recoveredFromDocumentation` so a null type reads as *not published* rather than *not worked out*. |
| B196 ✅ | **The diagram tools give an agent no way to see what it drew** | MCP | ⭐⭐ | M | The diagram tools return a description, so the agent is laying out a picture it cannot look at. Rendering the SVG to **PNG** and returning it would let the agent judge its own layout — and the SVG is already produced, so the question is which conversion to take on (and what it costs in dependencies). Alongside that, the guidance the tools give needs to say how connector positions should be chosen to match MSL and the usual conventions, and it is worth asking what else would make an automatically generated diagram look right. **Fixed 2026-09-23.** `get_diagram_image` renders the class - each component wearing its own type's icon at its `Placement`, `%name` resolved, the connection lines between them and the class's own diagram graphics - and returns a PNG. **The SVG was not in fact already produced**: `IconSvgRenderer` draws one class's icon, and composing a diagram out of those is the larger half of the item. `ModelicaParser/Icons/DiagramSvgRenderer.cs` does that composition and `MLQT.McpServer/Helpers/DiagramImage.cs` assembles it from the graph, reusing `DiagramGeometry`'s placement reader and connection router so the picture and `get_diagram_layout` cannot disagree - the regex for a `Placement` now has one home rather than three. Rasterised with **Svg.Skia** (MIT, on SkiaSharp), which is the dependency the row asked about; its native library is per-RID and is **not loaded until a diagram is asked for**, so `publish-tools.sh` checks it is in the tree - a tree missing it publishes, installs and answers every other tool, which is B133 and B144's shape. **Two deliberate departures from a Modelica viewer**: nothing is clipped (the view grows to hold what was placed outside the canvas, and the declared canvas is outlined), and a component whose type is not loaded is drawn as a dashed named box, because *not loaded* and *not there* must not look alike. The guidance half is a new `get_guidance("diagrams")` topic: sizes and the 10-unit grid, 20x20 blocks with 20-30 between them, left-to-right signal flow, which edge each kind of connector sits on, what rotation does to them, and *copy the library you are in*. The topic list and the topic texts are now held to each other by a test, having been a list and a dictionary of the same thing. **Corrected 2026-09-23**, from the first render put beside Dymola's: `Modelica.Blocks.Continuous.Integrator` came out as two oversized triangles overlapping in the middle of an empty canvas where Dymola draws four connectors round the edge. Three defects in one picture, none visible without it. A Placement's `extent` is stated **relative to its `origin`**, and reading the extent alone puts every component that uses one at the centre of the diagram. Only components the class **declares** were drawn, and a Modelica block usually declares no connector at all - `u` and `y` come from `Interfaces.SISO`. And a **connector is drawn with its Diagram layer, not its Icon layer**: Modelica gives it both because they answer different questions, and `RealInput`'s icon is a triangle filling its whole coordinate system where its diagram layer is a smaller one against the edge carrying a `%name` label. All three lived in `DiagramGeometry`'s placement reader and `DiagramImage`, so the connection router was wrong the same way; `get_diagram_layout` had a **fourth copy** of the regex and reported the centre-box extents the image used to draw, which is the tool and its sibling disagreeing about one diagram. It now reads through the same primitive. Nine tests, each confirmed by putting its defect back. **Corrected again the same day**, from the second render put beside Dymola's - `Modelica.Blocks.Examples.PID_Controller` - and four more defects, three of them reported by the user and the fourth found beside them. `lineThickness` and `arrowSize` are stated in **millimetres**, a length on the page and not in the drawing, so the coordinate units they come to depend on how far the view is zoomed: one constant gave a 24 px tree icon a visible hairline and a 1200 px diagram a ten-pixel outline, which is what the red frames came out as. A `Line`'s **arrow** was never drawn at all. The regex reading a connect's `points={{..},{..}}` was `\{(.*?)\}\s*\}`, which lazily stops at the `}}` closing the **last** point - so a six-point route was drawn with five and a two-point one had a single point, failed the `Count < 2` guard and was silently re-routed. And a component draws **its own type's connectors on its icon**, at their `iconTransformation` or, where there is none - which is most of MSL - at their `transformation`; without them a diagram is a row of boxes with lines ending near them. The fourth: a reversed extent mirrors a component, and the mirroring was reaching its **text**, so `speedSensor` and `loadTorque` were labelled backwards. Two gaps left standing and written down rather than guessed at: **B277** (a conditional connector drawn although it is switched off) and **B278** (`%parameter` references in icon text). |
| B218 ✅ | **`get_class_source` hands an agent a rewrite when it strips annotations** | MCP | ⭐⭐ | S | With `includeAnnotations: false` the tool re-renders the class through `ModelicaRenderer`, so the text an agent reads is not the file and its line numbers do not match the findings the same server reports. B214's elision strips the annotations without touching anything else, and then they do. Small, independent of the viewer work, and it can be taken in any phase after B214. **Fixed 2026-09-22.** It now cuts the annotations out of the text and blanks the lines they had to themselves, so every line an agent reads is the file's own line at its own number and the `modelLine` of a finding indexes it directly - held by a test that reads a finding through `list_findings` and indexes `get_class_source` with it, annotations on and off. **One thing was not in the row**, and it is why this was not a one-line swap: the elision was built for the viewer, which never parses what it elides, so it takes the semicolon after an annotation away with it. That semicolon belongs to the annotation only in a class body; under a declaration, an extends clause or an external clause it closes *that*, and dropping it ran one declaration into the next. `ElisionFinder` now asks which, under `elidedTextMustParse` - left off for the viewer, because keeping the semicolon puts a line holding nothing but `;` on screen for every hidden annotation, which is exactly the noise B233 removed. |

### Revision control

| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B193 ✅ | **Switch Branch cannot reach a Git tag** | Git | ⭐⭐ | M | Switching to a tagged version of `ExternData` is not offered, although TortoiseGit does it on the same working copy. The branch selector presumably enumerates branches only; checking out a tag produces a detached HEAD, which the surrounding UI also has to be able to describe rather than showing an empty branch name. **✅ Done 2026-09-21.** `GetBranches` lists tags beside branches for Git, flagged `IsTag`; the selector groups them under a `tags` folder and chips them as tags; `SwitchBranch` falls back to a tag and checks out its commit. **Annotated tags were the easy thing to get wrong**: their ref points at a tag object rather than a commit, so anything reading `Target` directly loses exactly the tags a release process creates - `PeeledTarget` is the one that handles both, and a theory covers each kind. **The second half is the half that would have been left out**: checking out a tag detaches HEAD, so `GetCurrentBranch` correctly returns null and the browser showed a blank. `GetDetachedHeadLabel` answers where HEAD is - the tag if it is on one, otherwise a short commit id - and the header now reads *“Detached HEAD at v2.0.0”*. Offering a switch into a state the application cannot describe would have been worse than not offering it. The dialog says what will happen **before** the switch rather than after. SVN needed nothing: its tags are directories and have always arrived as `tags/*`, and a test says so, so the new grouping cannot quietly take that path over. Eighteen tests across the VCS layer and the two components, each verified by mutation. **Reported on first use 2026-09-21: the dialog jumped to the width of the screen when a tag was clicked.** The content carried a `min-width` and no width of its own, MudDialog sizes to its content, and `MudDialogProvider`'s default cap is `MaxWidth.Large` - so the paragraph explaining detached HEAD, which appears on the click, stretched the dialog underneath the pointer that caused it. The content now has a width (`460px`, `max-width: 100%` for a narrow window) and the dialog is opened `MaxWidth.Small`. Three tests: a headless render cannot measure a layout, but it can hold that the declaration is there and that clicking a tag still produces the alert - between them the reason it used to resize. |
| B202 ✅ | **The VCS History file diff compares against the working copy** | Git / SVN | ⭐⭐ | S | Clicking a file in a commit's changed-files popover opens a diff of that revision against the **working copy**. What a user reviewing history wants is what that commit *changed* — the selected commit against its predecessor. B155 corrected the documentation of the current behaviour after finding it reported confusing numbers; this changes the behaviour to the one the dialog is for. **✅ Done 2026-09-21.** The dialog now shows the commit's predecessor on the left and the commit on the right. **The predecessor is a VCS question, not arithmetic for the caller**: Git has no ordering to count back along - it is the commit's *first* parent, and for a merge any other parent reports the lines the merge brought over as though the merge had written them - while SVN records no parents at all (`VcsLogEntry.ParentRevisions` is empty for it) but numbers revisions globally, so N-1 is the state of the repository before N whether or not N-1 touched the file. So `GetPreviousRevision` is on `IRevisionControlSystem`, answered once per system. Null means nothing came before, and the caller reads that as an empty left-hand side rather than a failure - the first commit of a repository and a file the commit added reach the same place by different routes. **The four existing tests passed unaltered against the new behaviour** - `SidesFor` takes two strings and does not know what they mean - while their names and prose described a dialog that no longer existed, so they were rewritten rather than extended. Twelve new tests, and the merge one is the only one that can tell first parent from last: with a single parent, `FirstOrDefault` and `LastOrDefault` are the same mutant. |

### Test fidelity — from the whole-solution mutation audit

Opened 2026-09-19 from `build/run-mutation.ps1 -All` over all seven measured assemblies (5h 40m;
23,634 mutants run, 5,990 survived, 4,159 in `MLQT.Shared` code no test executes at all — which is
the coverage ratchet's existing, reasoned debt and not a finding here). What *is* a finding is
below, and it has one shape: **the invariants this repository documents most carefully are the ones
with no test behind them.** Each line cited carries a comment explaining why the guard matters, is
executed by the suite — so the coverage gate is satisfied — and can be deleted or inverted without a
single test objecting. Regenerate the survivor list with `-Summarise`; it is git-ignored.

**Re-run 2026-09-22, and the figures above are not comparable with it** — 52m 53s, 24,901 mutants,
11,343 survivors. **B267 is why**, and the change was the right one: before it, Stryker ran in
solution mode and judged a mutant by *every* suite that transitively references the mutated
assembly, so the projects at the bottom of the dependency graph were being marked by tests in
projects above them. The pre-B267 reports still on disk prove it — `ModelicaGraph` was mutated
against **3,218** tests where its own suite has 995, and `MLQT.Services` against **2,305**. Only
`MLQT.Cli` and `MLQT.McpServer` had the same suite either way (nothing references them), so only
those two rows can be set side by side. Everything else got harder to pass, which is the point: the
question is whether *this assembly's own tests* check it.

**The per-project result, separating code no test runs from code the tests run and do not check** —
the second is the number worth acting on, and `build/survivor-map.py` groups it by method:

| Project | Mutants | Unreached | Reached | Survived | Kill on reached |
|---------|--------:|----------:|--------:|---------:|----------------:|
| ModelicaParser | 7,034 | 250 | 6,784 | 1,229 | **81.9%** |
| ModelicaGraph | 2,512 | 196 | 2,316 | 620 | 73.2% |
| RevisionControl | 1,606 | 340 | 1,266 | 383 | 69.7% |
| MLQT.Cli | 1,360 | 0 | 1,360 | 438 | 67.8% |
| MLQT.Services | 3,756 | 535 | 3,221 | 1,039 | 67.7% |
| MLQT.McpServer | 2,630 | 356 | 2,274 | 847 | 62.8% |
| MLQT.Shared | 6,003 | **4,375** | 1,628 | 735 | 54.9% |

`MLQT.Shared`'s 4,375 unreached is the coverage ratchet's existing, reasoned debt and not a finding
(and the browser journeys, which reach a great deal of it, are not in a mutation run at all). Its
14.9% headline is that debt; 54.9% is what its own tests do with what they execute.

**The two worst ratios in the whole campaign are non-findings**, checked before being reported:
`MLQT.Shared/Models/AppSettings.cs` (152 survived, 7 killed) is 127 mutations of **hex colour
literals** in theme presets, and `MLQT.McpServer/Helpers/ConnectorColor.cs` (40 survived, 1 killed)
is 40 string mutations in a colour table. Both are the "code nobody should test" shape CLAUDE.md
names. Reading the ratio alone would have opened two items worth nothing.


| # | Item | Area | Value | Effort | What is needed |
|---|------|------|-------|--------|----------------|
| B219 ✅ | **Nothing notices if an external-stub write/report guard is removed** | Checking | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | `LibraryCheckSession.cs:44` and `FormattingPipeline.cs:144-145` (`SourceType != EncryptedDirectory`, `!ReferenceOnlyScope.IsReference`) could each be inverted with nothing objecting — every test asserted the *included* outcome, so the thing that should have been excluded was never in the list. `ExternalStubWriteGuardTests` now asserts the excluded outcome with its positive control in the same test, and the three `Where` clauses moved to `FormattableLibraries` so the write guard is answerable without running a hundred-line save. Two corrections to the original row: **`GraphAnalysisContext.cs:47` does not belong here** — its survivor forces the filtering branch, which filters a list with nothing to filter, and the guard itself is tested; and `LibraryCheckSession.cs:44` **still survives**, because `StyleCheckRunner` filters stubs again downstream, so removing the first of two filters changes no observable result. |
| B220 ✅ | **The formatting-exclusion decisions are covered but unchecked** | Rules / formatting | ⭐⭐ | S | One of the three was real. `StyleCheckingSettings.cs:310` caches the compiled exclusion patterns, and the mutation making it ignore an edit survived `EditingTheListTakesEffect` — because that test goes empty → one → empty and `IsLibraryExcluded` returns on an empty list before the cache is consulted. Replacing one entry with another is what reaches it, and is what a user does when correcting a name in Settings; three tests now cover replace, grow and shrink, and the mutant is dead. **The other two were equivalent mutants**: `FormattingExclusion.cs:57` and `CoverageDimensions.cs:147` are short-circuits in front of a slower answer that agrees, so forcing the slow path changes nothing. Recorded because "a survivor is a missing test" is the wrong reading, and this row asserted it. |
| B221 ✅ | **Nothing notices if a parse diagnostic becomes suppressible or baselineable** | MCP / CLI | ⭐⭐ | S | "Always reported, never configurable, never baselined" was enforced in four places and tested in none. `SuppressionTools.cs:64` refused a diagnostic and no test ever passed it one, so both the `\|\|` and the `"MLQT."` prefix could be emptied — a theory over five ids, bare and prefixed, now asserts the refusal *and* that the source is untouched. `StyleTools.cs:286` passes `honorSuppressions: true`; flipping it made check_library report findings the user had waived, which is worse than noise because an agent will go and "fix" them. `MetricsRecorder.cs:92` excluded diagnostics from the recorded debt count, and the `&&`→`\|\|` mutant survived because no test had a diagnostic in the set. All four mutants confirmed dead. `Baseline.cs:191/267` is left open: its survivors change whether an empty `ExcludedLibraries` is written as `[]` or omitted, which is a serialisation shape rather than a guard. |
| B222 ✅ | **`ImpactAnalysisService` computed an SVG layout nothing read** | Dependencies | ⭐⭐ | S | Deleted: `CalculateForceDirectedPositions` and `CalculateEdgeEndpoints` (about 165 lines of circular placement, deterministic jitter, a 50-iteration force relaxation and clamping), `NetworkNode.X`/`.Y`, `NetworkEdge.X1`–`Y2`, `ImpactAnalysisResult.SvgWidth`/`.SvgHeight` and the two `svgWidth`/`svgHeight` parameters — 243 lines net. **Nothing in the product referenced any of them**: the build broke only in the test project, which is the proof. The three tests that kept it alive are gone too; each looped over a collection asserting a bound the code could not violate (`node.X > 0` against a clamp of 40, `SvgWidth >= 700` against `Math.Max(700, ...)`) and would have passed on an empty result. Replaced by two that can fail — every edge end is a node in the same result, and the node set is exactly the selected plus impacted models, by count as well as identity. |
| B223 ✅ | **The read-only-settings guard on a repository is unchecked** | Repositories | ⭐⭐ | S | `RepositoryService.cs:634` sets `IsSettingsReadOnly` when `.mlqt/settings.json` cannot be written, and `:317` is the only thing that reads it — the warning telling the user their per-repository settings are being ignored. Both could be removed silently; both mutants are now dead. Tested by making the write fail portably (a *file* where the `.mlqt` directory must go, so `Directory.CreateDirectory` throws the `IOException` the guard catches) with an ordinary repository as the positive control. `:241` stays as it is: its survivor changes only whether a log line is emitted. |
| B224 ✅ | **The test suite committed to the MLQT repository itself** | Test harness | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Running `run-mutation.ps1` over `MLQT.Services` made `RepositoryServiceTests` create two branches in the MLQT checkout, commit to one as `MLQT User <user@mlqt.local>`, detach HEAD and discard every uncommitted change in the working tree — twice, on 2026-09-18 and again on 2026-09-19, the first time silently moving two days of commits onto a branch nobody created deliberately. The tests build a real git repository under the temp directory and are correct; what was missing was any statement that the service still had hold of it. A string in `RepositoryService` mutated to `""`, git fell back to the process working directory, and that is the checkout. **This generalises**: any mutated code doing file or VCS work can act on the repository the run started in. Fixed by `SandboxedId`, which proves the repository is under `Path.GetTempPath()` before returning its id, on all 61 call sites, with `SandboxedId_RefusesARepositoryOutsideTheTempDirectory` as its positive control. The SVN tests against a fixed path outside the sandbox are the deliberate exception and stay unguarded — recorded at the helper. |
| B225 ✅ | **`--force-with-lease` was promised in two interfaces and pinned by nothing** | Git | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | `GitRevisionControlSystem.cs:1597` builds `push --force-with-lease`, `IRevisionControlSystem.ForcePush` and `IRepositoryService.ForcePushAsync` both document the lease, and the string appeared in **no test in the solution** — the mutation audit could replace it wholesale unnoticed. Dropping to a plain `--force` passes every other test here and silently deletes a colleague’s pushed commits the first time two people share a branch. Now tested as behaviour rather than as a flag: with a bare remote and a second clone that pushes first, the force push must fail **and their commit must still be on the remote** — the second half is what a plain `--force` breaks. A positive control covers the un-diverged case, and swapping the flag was confirmed to fail the test. |
| B226 ✅ | **The formatter could overwrite a file it had failed to parse** | Formatting | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | `IncrementalFormatter.cs:143` returns rather than writing when the render reported parse errors — "reformatting invalid Modelica produces unreliable output, and this overwrites the file in place". Deleting that `return` broke no test: nothing had ever handed `FormatAndWriteAsync` a file it could not parse, on the path that runs **at startup and after every VCS operation**. `IncrementalFormatterWriteTests` now covers it, with the formatted case as its positive control and a third test holding the scope of the guard — one unparseable file must not stop the others being formatted, or a single syntax error silently switches formatting off across the repository. |
| B227 ✅ | **`ModelicaRenderer`: 361 surviving mutants, and it rewrites every line of a user’s library** | Formatting | ⭐⭐ | L | 2,393 mutants run, 85% killed — a good rate over much the largest body of decisions in the solution, and still the biggest absolute count anywhere in the audit. Each survivor is one layout decision the suite executes and does not depend on, in the code that rewrites a library in place. Not a defect list and not a score to chase: the work is to read them in groups (indentation state, section ordering, annotation placement, line breaking) and decide per group whether the behaviour is specified anywhere. Expect a meaningful share to be equivalent — two spellings of the same output. **Do not start this without a way to sample**; 361 read end to end is how a pass like this turns into tests that assert the renderer’s current output rather than its contract. **✅ Done 2026-09-22, and the answer to “is this behaviour specified?” was mostly no.** The sampling the row insisted on is `build/survivor-map.py`, which groups a Stryker report’s survivors by the method they sit in — so a group can be judged as a whole instead of 272 mutants being read end to end. Re-measured first, because this row’s figures were from the WP8 audit: **272 survivors at 85.38%**, not 361. The map put 84 of them in four methods. Of those, **two groups had something written down to test against** and were taken: the file’s shape (`VisitStored_definition` — the within clause, a blank line between classes and none after the last) and section merging and ordering (`VisitComposition`/`WriteComposition`/`WriteInitialSections`, all six switches of which `Documentation/code-formatting.md` describes). **The largest single group was left deliberately**: the six-condition heuristic in `VisitClass_or_inheritence_modification` that decides when a modification goes multi-line counts arguments and nesting depth against thresholds nobody chose (`> 5`, `>= 2`), and a test for those would freeze today’s bytes under the name of a contract — which is the failure this row was written to prevent. Only its one stated rule, *break a line that would exceed the maximum*, is now held. **Why the file-shape group was unguarded is the harness, not an oversight**: `TestHelpers.AssertClass` ends with `actualOutput.RemoveAt(0)` and strips trailing blank lines, so every renderer test written through it discards the within clause and the blank lines before asserting; and it has no parameter for `InitialSectionsLast`, so no test could set that option at all. The new tests render directly. **Two real findings**: B273 (`within ;`), and six `WriteComposition` call sites passing an argument that is only ever read for a protected section — removed, with the one protected call that does need it named at the call site. Measured: **272 → 256 survivors, 85.38% → 86.01%**. The small move is the honest result: what is left in `VisitStored_definition` is index arithmetic over a shape the grammar guarantees, and most of the rest is layout nobody has ever specified. |
| B228 ✅ | **`ModelicaFileEncoding` loses a byte-order mark, and can ignore an explicit encoding** | Parser | ⭐⭐ | S | Four survivors that are not equivalent. `:132` inverts `!File.Exists`, so an existing file is written as `Default` instead of in the encoding it was read in — harmless for the bytes, because the fast path is only taken for ASCII text, but a **UTF-8 BOM is dropped**, which is a change to a file MLQT was asked to preserve. `:112`, `:168` and `:175` are `encoding ?? …` null-coalescing mutations: an explicit `encoding` argument can be discarded with no test objecting. The `IsAscii` survivors on `:154`/`:158` are **not** part of this — they push onto the slower path that agrees, exactly as the method’s own comment says. **✅ Done 2026-09-21, and the row's line numbers were stale - the survivors were re-read from the tool B267 had just fixed rather than trusted.** Two of the four named were still alive: the `!File.Exists` guard in `EncodingToWrite` (`:250`), where an existing file takes the new-file answer and a UTF-8 BOM is silently dropped, and the **synchronous** `encoding ??` (`:114`) - the asynchronous half was already covered by `AnExplicitEncoding_IsUsedInsteadOfTheFilesOwn`, and only that half.
