The third `??` is a *newline* rather than an encoding one and was already dead, because every write goes through `ForFile`. **The real gap was one line further in**: `DominantNewline`'s counting loop had no coverage at all - its comparison, its increments and its `crlf > lf` answer could each be changed with nothing objecting - because nothing tested `ForFile` directly, only through the encoding tests, which never vary the endings. Measured either side on the same command: **36 survivors before, 26 after**, the ten killed being both targets plus the whole of that loop. What is left is equality mutations in `DetectFromBytes`'s byte comparisons and block removals in catch handlers, which is the shape the row said to expect. |
| B229 ✅ | **`DymolaCheckingService` and `OpenModelicaCheckingService` are the least-guarded code in the solution** | External tools | ⭐⭐ | M | 11% of covered mutants killed in each — 130 and 122 run, 116 and 108 surviving. They are the only two services whose suites **no CI job runs** (`DymolaInterface.Tests` and `OpenModelicaInterface.Tests` need a live tool), so nothing outside one developer’s machine exercises them at all, and the mutation audit measured what that is worth. These parse a tool’s output into findings the user then acts on, so a survivor here is a misreported check result rather than a cosmetic one. Needs a live Dymola or `omc`; `build/run-all-tests.ps1` is the only thing that runs them today. Note B116 is already open against `omc` 1.26 on the same suite. **✅ Done 2026-09-21, and it needed no live tool at all.** The premise was that these are “the only two services whose suites no CI job runs” — but the services live in `MLQT.Services` and are tested by `MLQT.Services.Tests`, which CI does run. It is `DymolaInterface.Tests` and `OpenModelicaInterface.Tests`, the *interface* suites, that need a tool. **What actually stopped the services being tested was a type in a signature**: both factories returned the concrete session class, every method of which ends in a round trip to a running tool, so nothing past the first call was reachable without one. Extracting `IDymolaInterface` (5 members) and `IOpenModelicaInterface` (4) — exactly what the checking services call — made the whole of both reachable. **The tests are one contract run against both tools** (`MLQT.Services.Tests/ModelCheckingServiceContract.cs`, 48 behaviours × 2, with `ToolHarness.cs` holding fakes that model each tool’s **log buffer** rather than returning fixed strings — most of these promises are about which check’s output a result carries, and a stub cannot tell a drained buffer from an undrained one). Measured: **11% killed became 73.6% and 73.5%, and 176 mutants covered by no test became 13**. **It found two defects, both in both tools** (B272): the package path had its own copy of the check body, without the drain-the-log-first step or the read-it-on-success step, so B170’s warnings were shown when you checked one class and lost when you checked its package, and an error could be reported against the class after the one that produced it. Fixed by making `CheckSingleModelAsync` the only place a check happens. Of the 52 survivors left, 36 are log-call text and the other 16 are throttle timing and two equivalent null checks — read and recorded at the test class, as CLAUDE.md asks. |
| B255 ✅ | **Two WP3 tests only held on the platform they were written on, and CI is where that showed** | Testing | ⭐⭐ | S | The Linux `build-libraries` job failed on the push at the end of WP3; both Windows jobs and everything else were green. **Two tests, one shape**: an assumption about the machine, invisible on the machine that made it. (1) `ModelicaPackageSaverTests.SaveLibraryToDirectoryWithResult_DoesNotReformatAnExcludedModel_EvenWhenFormattingWouldChangeIt` asserted the written file was **CRLF throughout**, and its own comment explained why - the fixture is a raw string literal, so it carries the endings of the test file it sits in. Those are CRLF on a Windows checkout and LF on the runner, where `core.autocrlf` is off. **The product was right on both**; the test named an ending B251 had deliberately made the platform's business. Now asserts the property B251 actually claims - one ending throughout, whichever - through a helper that says nothing about which. (2) `PackageSplitterTests.IfTheOldFileCannotBeDeleted_TheUserIsToldTheyHaveItTwice` made the delete fail by holding the file with `FileShare.None`. **Four other tests in this repository do the same thing and are right to**: .NET implements `FileShare` on Unix as an advisory lock that other .NET `FileStream`s honour, so blocking a *read* works on both platforms. Deleting is not a read - `unlink` ignores advisory locks, so the delete simply succeeded and the test asserted failure where the product reported success, which is the one outcome that test exists to catch. It now puts a directory in the file's place, which makes `File.Delete` throw everywhere; the splitter takes the path from the graph rather than from disk, so nothing before the delete notices. **Neither could be reproduced locally** - no WSL or Docker on the development machine - and an attempt to simulate the first by converting the test file to LF did *not* reproduce it, which is worth knowing: the simulation was wrong and the fix had to be made unconditionally true instead of true-on-both-measured-platforms. |
| B256 ✅ | **`ubuntu-latest` becomes Ubuntu 26 on 19 October 2026, and Playwright ships no browser for it** | CI / testing | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Flagged by an annotation on every Linux job in the run of 2026-09-20: *"The ubuntu-latest label will migrate to Ubuntu 26 beginning October 19, 2026."* Six jobs across three workflows use the floating label - `linux-tests`, both `ubuntu-latest` legs of the `ui-journeys` and `desktop-self-test` matrices, `nightly-webkit`, and the `.deb` build in `release.yml`. **This is not a routine image bump, and the analysis is already written down**: `skill-gui-testing.md` records that Playwright ships no browser build for Ubuntu 26.04 at all - `install` refuses outright and the newest platform it knows is `ubuntu24.04-x64` - and that with `PLAYWRIGHT_HOST_PLATFORM_OVERRIDE=ubuntu24.04-x64` Chromium runs and **WebKit still will not launch**, because its build links `libicu74` and `libvpx9` while 26.04 ships `libicu78` and no `libvpx9`. That was measured for B135 on a developer machine; the date turns it into a CI problem. **The nightly WebKit rehearsal is the one that has no workaround** - it exists precisely because WebKitGTK is what the Linux desktop host runs on, so losing it means the Linux GUI is rehearsed nowhere. `build/run-all-tests.ps1` already detects a too-new Ubuntu and applies the override, held by `TestRunnerScriptTests`; **no workflow does**, so CI has none of that protection. Three decisions, and they are separate: pin the runner image to `ubuntu-24.04` (buys time, and the label is the only thing that moves - but a pin nobody revisits is how a project ends up on an unsupported image); apply the override in the workflows as the script does, which keeps Chromium and not WebKit; or find what WebKit needs on 26.04. **Also check the `.deb`**, which is a different question with the same trigger: built on 26 it would declare dependencies against that release's libraries, and MLQT's users are not all on it. **Do this before 19 October, not after** - the failure arrives on a date rather than on a commit, so it will land on whatever push happens to be next and will read as that change having broken something. **✅ Pinned 2026-09-21, four weeks before the date.** All six jobs across the three workflows now name `ubuntu-24.04`, and `WorkflowPlatformParityTests.NoJobRunsOnTheFloatingUbuntuLabel` refuses a new one that goes back to the label - the pin is the kind that holds until somebody adds a job, and the failure it would cause arrives on a date rather than on that commit, so it would land on whatever push happened to be next and read as that change having broken something. Two existing parity assertions had the label written into them and now look for an Ubuntu runner rather than a particular version, so repinning does not break them. **The choice was the pin rather than the override**, which keeps Chromium and cannot bring WebKit back, or a fix for 26.04, which nobody has: the object is to finish the backlog and cut a release, and the pin is what makes both possible without changing what is rehearsed. It is not a fix - revisit when Playwright ships a 26.04 browser, or by April 2029 when 24.04 leaves standard support, whichever comes first. **The `.deb` needed nothing.** Its `Depends:` is hand-written and unversioned - `package-deb.sh` refuses `dpkg-shlibdeps` so the package is not pinned to its build host - nothing in the payload is compiled there, and the archive is `-Zxz` rather than the host default, so the floor is `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` + GTK3 (Ubuntu 22.04, Debian 12) wherever it is built. What a newer builder would have cost is not the package but the proof: the release job extracts what it built and runs the 16 `/selftest` probes against it, and that needs a WebKitGTK that launches. |
| B267 ✅ | **`run-mutation.ps1` cannot mutate `ModelicaParser` any more: its baseline run includes the browser journeys** | Test harness | ⭐⭐ | S | Found on 2026-09-21 doing WP7. `./build/run-mutation.ps1 -Project ModelicaParser -Mutate '**/Comparison/*.cs'` never reaches the mutation phase: Stryker runs an initial test run of **5,790 tests** — more than `ModelicaParser.Tests`'s 2,084 — and aborts with *"Initial testrun has more than 50% failing tests"* naming `MLQT.Journeys.TabNavigationJourney.MovingThroughEveryTabAndBack_RaisesNothing` as `error`. **That journey passes on its own** (`dotnet test MLQT.Journeys -c Release --filter TabNavigationJourney`, 7 passed), so this is about the run, not the journey. The cause looks structural rather than environmental: Stryker is in solution mode (*"Identifying projects to mutate in MLQT.slnx"*), and in solution mode it runs **every test project that transitively references the mutated one** — which for `ModelicaParser` is all of them, journeys included — apparently regardless of the `--test-project` the script already passes. **The arithmetic confirms it**: 5,790 is the seven core suites' 6,412 less `RevisionControl.Tests`'s 689 — the one suite that references nothing — plus the journeys. The journeys share one host, so running them alongside six other suites is exactly the shape CLAUDE.md warns about. **WP9, and first in it**: B227 and B228 are lists of surviving mutants and every closure in B219-B226 was recorded as "mutant confirmed dead", which is a claim the tool has to be able to make. **Two candidate fixes, and which one is right is the question**: give Stryker the project file rather than the solution so it stops enumerating, or teach the script to exclude the journeys from the test set. Until then the audit and every per-file run over `ModelicaParser`, `MLQT.Services` or `MLQT.Shared` is unavailable, which is the tool CLAUDE.md names as the answer to "do the tests actually check anything?". **✅ Fixed 2026-09-21, and neither candidate above was quite right.** A solution cannot be taken away by argument, only by not standing where it can be found: Stryker walks up from its working directory, and from the repository root it finds `MLQT.slnx`. `Invoke-Stryker` now runs from the test project's own directory, where no solution is found, Stryker takes the single-project path and uses the suite it was started from. Measured on the same command that failed: *Analyzing 1 test project(s)*, **2,078 tests rather than 5,790**, and a mutation score in 2m32s where it previously aborted after 3m30s. `--test-project` is **removed** rather than kept in case - it did nothing in the case that mattered, and a flag that appears to select the suite while the working directory actually selects it is worse than no flag. The output path is resolved to an absolute one before the directory changes, or a relative `-Output` would land inside the test project. `-All` uses the same function and is unchanged; it was not re-run, because it takes hours and the path it exercises is the one just measured. |
| B268 ✅ | **A superseded library goes on claiming the classes it no longer supplies** | Libraries | ⭐⭐ | M | **Done 2026-09-23 (WP15), by a fourth option rather than any of the three listed**: none of those stopped the two copies being *mixed*, and that is the part that was wrong. The copies are routinely different releases, so merging them class by class kept a stub for every class the newer source had deleted — inside the user's own library, in the tree, and resolving a reference that should have been reported as broken. **Now an encrypted library is never loaded beside readable source for the same library, and the source wins whole.** One rule, `SourceSupersedesEncrypted` (exact top-level name; an unknown name matches nothing), applied twice: `RepositoryService.LoadLibrariesAsync` skips the encrypted build before reading it, from the names every repository's discovery already has — so the parallel load cannot race — and `LibraryDataService.Register`, which every load path now goes through, retires whichever copy registers second, which covers the reference-library setting, a library added mid-session and a folder not named after its library. Both are logged, not shown. **`RemoveLibrary` now removes only the nodes the library supplies**: it removed every id it listed, and an encrypted library that had lost classes to source still listed them, so removing the vendor copy deleted the user's classes from the graph. The three remaining `Libraries.FirstOrDefault(l => l.ModelIds.Contains(id))` searches in Code Review and the two in `LibraryDataService` go through `LibraryOwnership.Owner`, and **`LibraryOwnershipPolicyTests` is the guard**: every read of a library's `ModelIds` in production code is in a ledger with a reason, as `ModelicaFileAccessPolicyTests` does for file access — checked by putting one search back and watching it fail. Removing a repository can leave unloaded an encrypted build it was standing in for; the Manage Repositories tab then offers **Load project** on the active project (`IRepositoryService.RepositoryRemovedSinceProjectLoad`), which is what brought B280 to light. No startup cost to measure: nothing is serialised, and the skipped library is one never read. `LibraryOwnership` and `TotalModelCount`'s distinct count are now safety nets rather than the mechanism, and are kept — two readable checkouts of one library still produce two claimants. **Follow-ups the same day:** the loader asks before reading an encrypted library's help HTML, so the CLI's `--dependency` and the MCP server's `load_library` — which have no discovery pass in front — skip it too; `LoadedLibrary.SupersededBy` says why a library came back empty, because `load_library` had started calling such a library one that "ships no usable documentation"; and the CLI no longer names it as loaded or records it as a baseline dependency, with `Baseline.DriftFrom` told not to report one satisfied by source as missing, so a baseline taken before this change does not tell the user to pass a `--dependency` they already passed. |
| B271 ✅ | **`CodeReview.OnModelSelected` calls `StateHasChanged` off the dispatcher** | Code Review | ⭐⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-21 while doing B237, by the journey that item exists to make possible - which is the argument for the journey rather than against it. Under the journeys the circuit dies with `System.InvalidOperationException: The current thread is not associated with the Dispatcher. Use InvokeAsync() to switch execution to the Dispatcher when triggering rendering or component state.`, and the frame below the assert is `CodeReview.OnModelSelected` at `CodeReview.razor.cs:859` - the bare `StateHasChanged()` after `_isLoadingCode = true`. The same method marshals correctly a few lines above, inside the VCS branch, so the two halves of one method disagree. **The first question is whether a user can reach it**, and it is not answered: the stack arrives through `RendererSynchronizationContext.PostAsync`, so this may only be a continuation resuming after the circuit was already in trouble rather than the first fault. Settle that before changing anything - `OnModelSelected` is reached from the findings row click (on the dispatcher) and from `AppState.OnChangeModel`, and the second is raised from background analysis completions, which is the path that would make it real. CLAUDE.md already states the rule this is on the wrong side of: *in Razor event handlers, use `await InvokeAsync(StateHasChanged)`*. **✅ Withdrawn 2026-09-21: not a defect, and the row above it was wrong.** Filed from the stack alone before the reachability question it set itself had been answered - and the answer is no. `AppState` is a singleton shared by every circuit, the journeys opened 71 pages and closed none of them, so every stale page's `CodeReview` reacted to a model change raised by a later page **on that page's dispatcher**. The bare `StateHasChanged()` calls at `:824` and `:859` are the method's synchronous prologue and are correct where there is one circuit: the desktop host has one, and every `ChangeModelID` caller is on the dispatcher or wrapped in `InvokeAsync` already. Nothing in `CodeReview` was changed. The fix was in the harness (`NewPageAsync` closes the previous page) and it is B237's. **Kept rather than deleted because the mistake is the point**: a stack trace says where a thing failed, not whether a user can reach it, and this one was filed as a product defect on that evidence. |
| B272 ✅ | **A package check lost the warnings a single-class check showed, in both tools** | External tools | ⭐⭐ | S | Found by B229’s contract tests, fixed with them, recorded because the shape recurs. `CheckModelAsync` and `CheckSingleModelAsync` were two copies of the same check body in each service, and the copy the **package** path used had neither the clear-the-log-first step nor the read-the-log-on-success step. So `checkModel` returning true with six warnings — the exact case B170 exists for — surfaced them for a class checked on its own and dropped them for the same class checked as part of its package; and because the log was never drained between classes, an error reported against one class could be the one before it produced (Dymola’s log accumulates; omc’s empties on read, so there it leaked from a class that passed silently into the next one that failed). `CodeReview.razor.cs`’s `ReportedChecks` was already written for the fixed behaviour — it lists *“any clean check the tool still had something to say about”* — so the feature had been specified, shipped and never delivered on that path. **Neither half is visible from either path alone**, which is why the guard is a contract asserted once and run against both tools rather than a test per service. |
| B273 ✅ | **Every top-level `package.mo` MLQT wrote said `within ;`** | Formatting | ⭐⭐ | S | Found by B227’s first new test and fixed with it. `WithinClause` is, per CLAUDE.md, **the only place that adds or removes a leading within clause**, and it writes `within;` for a top-level file. The renderer wrote `within ;` — `Space()` was called before the optional name rather than with it — and `ModelicaPackageSaver` re-renders the whole `stored_definition` on every save, so the renderer’s spelling is the one that reached disk and the helper’s never did. Valid Modelica and stable (it re-parses to itself, so no churn loop), but it is a one-character diff on the top-level `package.mo` of every library the first time MLQT formats it, and a silent divergence from the single place the rule was supposed to live. **Nothing saw it because the only assertion about a within clause anywhere was `Assert.Contains("within", ...)`** — which the name, the space and the semicolon can all go missing under — and because `TestHelpers.AssertClass` deletes the within line before comparing. |
| B274 ✅ | **The MCP edit tools move and delete classes in a user's repository, and 319 mutants say nothing checks how** | MCP | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | From the 2026-09-22 campaign, by the second question WP8's audit asked — *which survivors sit on code that writes to a user's files?* `EditTools.cs` has **179 survivors** and `StructureEditTools.cs` **140**, and these are the operations an AI agent invokes to restructure somebody's library: `MoveClass` (40), `DeleteClass` (18), `DeleteDirectoryPackageAsync` (14), `MoveDirectoryPackageAsync` (13). The shape inside `StructureEditTools` is worse than the count: `RemoveComponent` (16) and `RemoveWholeLine` (10) survive mostly **equality and arithmetic** mutations — index arithmetic over the lines of a file that is about to be rewritten, where an off-by-one deletes the wrong line and the tests do not notice. B225 and B226 were this category and both were treated as same-day fixes; this is the same category, measured, and larger. **WP9.** **Start with the removal arithmetic, not the message strings** — a third of these are `String mutation` on tool responses, which matter much less than which line got deleted. **✅ Done 2026-09-22.** Two structural findings, both the duplication shape rather than a defect — the arithmetic was right, nothing depended on it. **(1) The line removal existed twice in one file**: `RemoveComponent`'s sole-on-line branch computed the same three offsets as `RemoveWholeLine` seven lines away, so the code deciding which characters of a user's file disappear had two copies and a fix to either was a fix to one of them. Now one, returning null where it used to fall back to `stop`, so `RemoveConnection` keeps the stricter answer `RemoveComponent` already gave. **(2) The subtree test existed four times**, always as `id == root \|\| id.StartsWith(root + ".")`, and the `"."` could be emptied in every copy — which makes `Root.Src` a prefix of `Root.SrcExtra` and sweeps an unrelated package into a move or a delete. All four now go through `ModelicaName.IsInSubtree`/`ReRoot`, **added to the helper that already existed for exactly this reason** and whose own summary already said so: *“three lines of string arithmetic, written out at every site that needed them… that is the sort of thing that stays right until one copy is edited.”* **The tests are the contract, not the count**: what removal leaves behind is *the original lines minus exactly one* (which catches swallowing the line above, leaving a blank line, and merging two lines — none of which change whether the removed text is gone, which was all the tests before them asked), and a refused move is asserted **by message** with the library unchanged, because `Move_Validation` asserted only `IsType<ToolError>` and a mutant that removes the guard still errors further down. Measured: `StructureEditTools` **140 → 128** survivors, `EditTools` 179 → 175 over 13 fewer mutants, and the relocated logic is **not** hiding in a blind spot — `ModelicaName`'s three new members have **zero** surviving mutants under `ModelicaParser.Tests`, which was checked rather than assumed. The message-string survivors are left, as the row asked. |
| B275 ✅ | **`Push`, `Commit` and `ForcePush` have 64 surviving mutants between them** | Revision control | ⭐⭐ | M | From the 2026-09-22 campaign. `GitRevisionControlSystem.cs` has **228 survivors** and `SvnRevisionControlSystem.cs` **139**, and the concentration is in the operations that change a user's history and their remote: `Push` (24), `Commit` (22), `ForcePush` (18), then `GetWorkingCopyChanges` (13) and `AbortRebase` (13). Unlike B274 these have a real reason behind them — `RevisionControl.Tests` is 689 tests against temporary LibGit2Sharp repositories and a local SVN server, and pushing needs a remote nothing has — so the honest split is **which of these can be tested against a bare repository on disk** (a push to a `file://` remote can; force-push and its refusals can) and which genuinely cannot. `SvnRevisionControlSystem` also carries **205 unreached** mutants, which is the three svn-dependent classes CI excludes and is already reasoned debt rather than a finding. **WP9**, after B274. Do the Git half first: it needs no server. **✅ Done 2026-09-22, and the split came out three ways rather than two.** **(1) Testable, and the gap was real.** `Push` had no positive test at all — an invalid path and a repository with no remote were the whole of it, so nothing established that a push puts anything anywhere — and no test that a **non-fast-forward push is refused and the other person's commit survives**, which is the safety property separating `push` from `push --force-with-lease` and which force-push already had a test for. Both now exist, against a bare repository on disk, using the fixture the force-push tests already established. `Push` **24 → 18** survivors. `Commit` gained a test that an untracked file really is in the commit — `Commit_WithAllChanges_StagesAndCommits` created one but asserted only that a commit came back, and it modified a tracked file too, so turning off `IncludeUntracked` left it green while every newly created class silently failed to be committed. **(2) One target turned out to be an equivalent mutant**, checked by hand rather than assumed: `RecurseUntrackedDirs = false` in the staging block changes nothing, because `Commands.Stage` on the directory path stages its contents whether or not the status listing expanded them. The test is kept — it states a real promise and it is what kills `IncludeUntracked` — but the mutant beside it cannot be killed and is recorded here rather than chased. **(3) What is genuinely left needs what no machine here has**: the rest of `Push`/`ForcePush` is error-message mapping from a `git` exit code, and authentication and network failures. `GitRevisionControlSystem` overall **228 → 221**. `SvnRevisionControlSystem` was not touched: its 139 survivors and 205 unreached mutants are the three svn-dependent classes CI excludes, which is reasoned debt already recorded in `build/coverage-baseline.json`. |
| B276 ✅ | **Ten more hand-written copies of “is this class inside that one?”** | Architecture | ⭐ | S | Found while doing B274, and deliberately out of its scope. The solution has **fourteen** sites spelling `id == root \|\| id.StartsWith(root + ".", StringComparison.Ordinal)`; B274 routed the four in `MLQT.McpServer/Tools/EditTools.cs` through `ModelicaName.IsInSubtree` and left the rest: `MLQT.Services/DymolaCheckingService.cs`, `MLQT.Services/OpenModelicaCheckingService.cs`, `MLQT.Shared/Pages/CodeReview.razor.cs`, `MLQT.Shared/Pages/MetricsDashboard.razor.cs` (×5) and `ModelicaGraph/Analysis/TypeResolver.cs` (×2). **Nothing is wrong today** — every copy is currently correct, and the two checking-service copies are held by `ModelCheckingServiceContract.CheckingAPackageChecksNeitherItselfNorItsNamesakes`. This is the shape this repository keeps rediscovering rather than a defect: a rule with fourteen implementations is a defect waiting for one of them to be edited. **Small, and a good first-thing-in-the-morning item**: the helper exists, the tests for it exist, and each site is a one-line substitution — but the two `MetricsDashboard` ones are scope filters where a namesake being included changes a number on screen, so check what each one means before replacing it rather than sweeping. **✅ Done 2026-09-22, and two of the ten were not substitutions at all.** All fourteen hand-written copies are gone — the only remaining match in production code is the sentence in `ModelicaName`'s own summary describing what it replaced. Three findings on the way: **(1) `TypeResolver`'s two sites were `IsInSubtree` and `ReRoot` fused**, not membership tests: each asked whether a name was under an import alias and then re-rooted it onto the import's target, in four lines that `ReRoot` does in one — `import SI = Modelica.SIunits;` making `SI.Voltage` mean `Modelica.SIunits.Voltage`. **(2) `MetricsDashboard` already had a helper**, `InScope`, carrying a remark that makes this item's whole argument — *“The dot matters. A scope of `Modelica.Blocks` covers `Modelica.Blocks.Sources.Sine` and must not cover `Modelica.BlocksExtra`”* — and **three of that file's five copies were asking the question inline instead of calling it**. So the row's “one-line substitution” was right for the count and wrong about the shape: the duplication was not only across files, it was around an existing helper in the same one. `InScope` now delegates to `IsInSubtree`, keeping its own empty-scope-means-everything rule, and the other four call it or `IsStrictlyInside`. **(3) `InScope` had no tests**, despite that remark; `MetricsScopeTests` gives it the namesake case it names, and mutation confirms **zero** survivors in it. The row's warning about the dashboard sites was worth having and the answer was cheap: what is left in that file is seven hex colour literals and one equivalent mutant (`IndexOf(ch, parent.Length + 1) < 0` to `<= 0` — the search starts past index 0, so it cannot return 0). The row also said “the two `MetricsDashboard` ones” in its advice while listing five; five is right. |
| B277 ✅ | **A conditional connector is drawn on a component's icon even when it is switched off** | MCP / diagrams | ⭐⭐ | M | Found 2026-09-23 comparing `get_diagram_image` on `Modelica.Blocks.Examples.PID_Controller` with Dymola's render of it, after the four defects the user reported were fixed. Four of the nine components show a port Dymola does not: `integrator` its `set` and `reset` (`if use_reset`), `torque` and `loadTorque` their `support` (`if useSupport`), and `spring` its `heatPort` (`if useHeatPort`) - the stray triangles outside the integrator and the dark red square under the spring. **Modelica declares a component conditional with `if <expr>`, and a tool draws it only when the expression is true**; all of these default to false, so every one of them is a port that does not exist. **Nothing in the repository records the condition at all**: `ClassElement` has no field for it and `ClassInterfaceExtractor` does not read the grammar's `condition_attribute`, so this is not a rendering fix - it is a parser DTO change plus an evaluation. The evaluation needed is small and bounded: the expression in practice is a boolean parameter name, or two joined by `and`, resolved against the instance's own modification (`spring(useHeatPort=true)`) and otherwise against the parameter's default in the type. **Draw it when the answer cannot be worked out**, not hide it: showing a port that is switched off is a smaller lie than hiding one that is switched on. Worth having beyond the picture - `get_class_interface` lists these connectors too, and tells an agent it can connect to a port that is not there. **Fixed 2026-09-23.** `ClassElement` now carries the `Condition` a component was declared with and `ClassInterfaceExtractor` reads the grammar's `condition_attribute`; `ModelicaCondition.Evaluate` answers it over names, `and`, `or`, `not` and the two literals, and **answers null for anything else** - which the renderer takes as *draw it*, for the reason the row gives. `ComponentValues` is the lookup both this and B278 needed: the instance's own modification, then the type's default including inherited ones, since the governing parameter is usually declared in a base class. The rule applies to an instance's **icon** and not to a class's own diagram, where a conditional connector is a declaration to be seen and edited and Dymola shows it too. `get_class_interface` and `list_class_elements` report the condition, so an agent is no longer told it can connect to a port that may not be there. **One thing was not in the row and is the reason it took a second attempt**: `ParserRuleContext.GetText()` concatenates token texts and drops the spaces between them, so `use_reset and use_set` came back as `use_resetanduse_set` - a single identifier resolving to nothing, which read as one more undecidable condition rather than as a bug, and left MSL's Integrator still showing a port after the other three had gone. The condition is read from the input stream as written. |
| B278 ✅ | **`%parameter` references in icon text are not substituted** | MCP / diagrams | ⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-23 alongside B277. MSL icons label themselves with the values they were given - `J=%J`, `c=%c`, `k=%k`, `deltaq=%deltaq`, `%controllerType` - and `get_diagram_image` renders those literally, so a diagram reads `J=%J` where Dymola reads `J=1 kg m2`. `%name` is already substituted, which is the same mechanism one step short: the value comes from the component's own modification where it has one (`inertia1(J=1)`) and from the parameter's default in the type otherwise, and `%%` is an escaped per cent. Smaller than B277 and shares its lookup - the instance modification, then the type's default - so the two are worth taking together. A diagram whose every parameter reads `%J` is legible but says nothing about the model in front of you, which is most of what a labelled icon is for. **Fixed 2026-09-23** with B277, whose lookup it shares. `IconText.Resolve` handles `%name`, `%class`, `%%` and `%<identifier>`, and **leaves an unknown name as written**: blanking it would say the parameter has no value, which a reader cannot tell from a tool that failed to find one. One judgement beyond the row: a value that is a **qualified name** is shown by its last segment, because MSL's PID example sets `controllerType=Modelica.Blocks.Types.SimpleController.PI` and Dymola labels the block `PI` rather than writing forty characters of package path across the diagram. **Not done**: Dymola also appends a parameter's unit (`J=1 kg m2`), which needs unit resolution and a presentation decision, and it truncates text to the icon's width, which we do not. |
| B279 ✅ | **`BranchSelector` reads the branch list twice every time it opens** | Code Review / VCS | ⭐⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-23 from the only CI failure of the WP10 push: `SwitchBranchDialogWidthTests` failed on the Windows runner and on nothing else, and passed when the same commit was re-run. `OnInitializedAsync` loads the branches, and the parameter set that follows it saw `_lastRepositoryId` still null and loaded them again — **two `GetBranches` calls into git or svn per dialog open**, and a second pass through the loading state, which blanks the tree that had just been drawn and puts it back. The flicker is too quick to see and was not too quick for a test that had found a row in the first tree and clicked it during the second load. The id is claimed in `LoadBranches` now, so the first parameter set sees the repository it is already showing and a genuine change still reloads. **The test was weak as well and is fixed with it**: it waited for the branch name to appear anywhere in the markup and then reached for the paragraph carrying it, which is a weaker condition than the one it depends on. Wait for the thing you are about to use. **Neither half reproduced locally** — twelve runs of the suite on the same machine, all green — which is what a race this narrow does, and why the mechanism was worth finding rather than the test worth re-running. |
| B280 ✅ | **Switching project dropped the Reference Libraries until the application restarted** | Startup | ⭐⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-23 doing B268, whose reload button runs this path. `SwitchProjectAsync` clears the whole graph and loads the new project's repositories; the libraries listed under **Settings → Reference Libraries** belong to no project, and only the startup path in `MainLayout` loaded them. So every project switch — and every reload of the active project — carried on without them: references into a tool's library folder stopped resolving, which reads as the user's code having broken references rather than as something missing. `OnProjectChanged` now loads them after the project's own libraries, as startup does, inside one tree announcement. |
| B281 ✅ | **The whole-graph analyses run one after another on one thread, and one of them reads the whole graph** | Performance | ⭐⭐ | M | Found 2026-09-24 profiling B261's follow-up on Claytex. Of a 257s check the whole-graph analyses are **72s, serial**: `UnusedMembersAnalyzer` 41s, `ShadowingAnalyzer` 23s, `UnusedImportAnalyzer` 9s, run in turn by `GraphAnalysisRunner.Run`'s `foreach`, each a plain loop — on a machine where the per-class phase before them uses 24 cores. `dotnet-trace` says both big ones are **~75% re-parsing** classes the per-class check has already released, and ~25% `ClassInterfaceExtractor`. **The larger defect is `UnusedMembersAnalyzer`'s first pass**: to learn which classes something extends it iterates `context.Graph.ModelNodes` — every class in the graph, 39,596 on Claytex with the Dymola folder loaded, not the 21,673 being checked — and parses each; the trace puts ~95% of the analyzer there and under 5% in the pass that checks the reported classes. That breaks the standing invariant that nothing may scale with graph size instead of checked-set size. Dependency analysis has run by then on every path that reaches this analyzer's rule, and it records `extends` as an edge, so "what extends this class" is already answered by `UsedByModelIds` for exactly the classes pass 2 considers — **verify that before relying on it**, and keep the parse as the fallback when edges are absent. Then parallelise within the analyzers (the per-class check already reads the same classes through `Borrow` from 24 threads), and only then ask whether running them concurrently is still worth it. Gate: findings identical on Claytex and MSL. **WP16**. **Done 2026-09-24, and the cause was not the one this row names.** The edge was verified first, as asked: over Claytex with the Dymola 2026x library folder all 16,226 extends clauses into the checked set carried an edge, the extended set coming out the same 2,264 classes either way. `UnusedMembersAnalyzer` then asks only the classes that use a candidate (the full scan kept as the fallback without edges) and both big analyzers run in parallel with their findings in the order given — **and the phase did not get faster**: 72s serial, 72s with twenty threads busy. The trace of the serial run had already said why, once read per thread: the thread running the analyses was suspended in `PollGCWorker` for **53 of its 72 seconds**. The phase was bound by garbage collection, and every host ran the default **workstation GC** — one heap, every thread stopped for every collection — while a check allocates parse trees from 24 cores. Measured, both builds under both collectors, findings identical in all four: before this change 242s workstation / **104s server**; with it **255s** workstation / **70.5s** server, peak memory 8.2 / 8.5 / 11.4 / 8.7 GB. So server GC is the gain (the analyzers fall to 7.4s and 4.7s under it), and the parallel analyzers are a loss without it — slower and 3 GB heavier. **All three hosts now set `ServerGarbageCollection`**, confirmed in each built `runtimeconfig.json` and in the published tree, whose smoke tests pass (16/16 self-test probes); `HostGarbageCollectionTests` holds it, read as XML and verified by mutation, because a host that lost the line would be worse off than before this work started. **The desktop app is the one host not measured directly** — its style checking is the same GC-bound work and should gain as much, but its memory and responsiveness over a long session want confirming on the next Claytex session. |
| B282 ✅ | **Dependency analysis parses every class in the graph twice** | Performance | ⭐⭐ | M | Found 2026-09-24 with B281. On Claytex dependency analysis is **74s** of a 257s check, and the application log of 2026-09-22 splits it: Phase 1+2 (parse, analyse, add edges) **42s** over 39,596 classes, then Phase 3, the second `loadSelector` pass, **40s** — parsing all 39,596 again, because `hasTrackedParams` is true if *any* class anywhere has a tracked `loadSelector`/`loadResource` parameter, and with a tool's library folder loaded one always does. A class can only modify such a parameter through something it uses, and Phase 1+2 has just built exactly those edges, so the candidates for Phase 3 are the classes that reach a class carrying a tracked parameter — how far that closure has to go (`extends` chains, a nested component's modifier `a.b.file=...`) is the part to work out from `LoadSelectorModificationAnalyzer` before narrowing anything. Gate: the external-resource graph identical on Claytex and MSL, resources and edges both. **WP16**, with B281. **Re-measure before starting**: the 74s was taken under workstation GC, and B281 found that collector was most of the graph analyses' time — dependency analysis parses the same way, so its figure has probably moved too, and the second parse may no longer be worth the narrowing. **Done 2026-09-24.** Re-measured first, as the row asked: under server GC dependency analysis was **19.2s** of a 69s Claytex check, not 74s, and Phase 3 about 8s of it — the collector had taken most of what this row was opened for. Two further guesses were tested and rejected on the way: the per-batch `GC.Collect(2)` calls (removing them measured 64.6–67.5s against 65.3–66.0s, noise) and the finalizer thread's 16s (the runtime's own array-pool and weak-table cleanup, not ours). **The narrowing itself held, and is narrower than the row feared**: the pass only matches `T comp(name = ...)` where `T` itself declares `name`, resolved by exact name or enclosing package, so a class can only produce a result by using a class that declares a tracked parameter. Verified before relying on it — over Claytex with the Dymola folder (2 producers), MSL (0) and Buildings with MSL (3), every producer was among those users; on Claytex they are **1,211 classes of 68,746**. `GraphBuilder.MayModifyATrackedParameter` asks that per class. **It also fixed a latent defect in the incremental path**, which ran the pass only if a re-analysed class itself *declared* a tracked parameter — so editing a class to set another class's file parameter lost that resource until the next full analysis; a test reproduced it failing before the fix. Gate: the external-resource graph identical, resources and edges, on all three corpora (27,741 / 4,555 / 10,338 lines); dependency analysis 23.7s → 15.5s on Claytex, 3.9s → 2.5s on MSL, 5.6s → 3.6s on Buildings; the whole Claytex check 68.8s → 65.6s (two runs each, ±3s noise); findings identical. |
| B283 ✅ | **Browsing for a folder could crash the desktop app, leaving nothing in the log** | Desktop host | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-24 while adding a repository: the folder dialog was open, the user was navigating it, and MLQT.Photino vanished. Nothing in MLQT's log; the Windows Application event log had `0x80000003` (a breakpoint — WebView2 stopping the process on purpose) in `EmbeddedBrowserWebView.dll` 153.0.4234.48. **The mechanism, from Photino.Blazor's source rather than guessed:** a click reaches a component through WebView2's `WebMessageReceived` callback, and `PhotinoWebViewManager` handles the message with `Task.Factory.StartNew(..., sts)` where `sts` is a `SynchronousTaskScheduler` whose `QueueTask` runs the task inline — so the component's handler, and `ShowOpenFolder` inside it, ran on the callback's own stack. The dialog's nested message loop was therefore running inside WebView2's event handler, and anything Blazor rendered while the user browsed reached `SendWebMessage` re-entrantly. The archive holds no earlier crash in that module (the seven from 8–9 September were `FileProviders` during the host migration), and runtime 153.0.4234.48 was installed on 20 September — consistent with a stricter runtime turning a long-standing re-entrancy into a stop, though not proof of it. **Fixed** by opening every dialog through `PhotinoFilePickerService.OnTheMessageLoopAsync`, which hands it to `PhotinoWindow.Invoke` from a pool thread so it runs from the top of the message loop after the callback has returned. `NativeDialogPolicyTests` holds every native dialog call in both Photino applications to it, verified by mutation; the published tree's 16 self-test probes pass. **Not reproducible by a test** — nothing automated can drive a native dialog — so the confirmation is the user browsing with the app busy. |
| B284 ✅ | **`__MLQT(format=false)` does not suppress *Components before classes* or *Declarations in order*; the name list does** | Rules / formatting | ⭐⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-24 reviewing the user documentation. `code-formatting.md` says the annotation “suppresses the same formatting-related rules” as the `FormattingExcludedModels` name list. It does not: the name list skips all nine layout rules in `StyleChecking.RunStyleCheckingFindings` (the `isExcludedFromFormatting` block), but `MlqtSuppressionExtractor.FormattingRuleIds` lists seven and omits `ComponentsBeforeClasses` and `DeclarationOrder`, both newer than the list (B252). So an annotated class still gets those findings. **Decided 2026-09-24: the code was wrong** — `format=false` waives every rule that would reformat a class or check its layout. **Fixed** by reading the list from the catalogue (every `Ordering` rule) instead of writing it out. The B72 guard `TheTwoFormattingExclusionsWaiveTheSameRules` already compared the two exclusions, and passed because its fixture broke neither newer rule, so the vacuity check beside it now requires the fixture to break every `Ordering` rule in the catalogue, not merely to report something. |
| B285 ✅ | **The Show/Hide Annotations button: which state is filled, and does it apply to the diff views?** | Code Review | ⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-24 reviewing the user documentation. `code-review.md` (Show/Hide Annotations row) says the button is filled while annotations are hidden and that hiding applies to the diff views too. The code fills it while they are *shown*, and the diff views always show the full source. Decide which is intended: a filled button usually means “this is on”, which makes the code's choice defensible if the toggle is read as *Show*; hiding annotations in a diff is harder, because it would have to elide both sides consistently. Then change whichever is wrong. **Decided 2026-09-24: the code is right on both** — filled means shown, and a diff shows the whole source. The documentation now says so. |
| B286 ✅ | **Dymola auto-detection skips Dymola 2020x** | External tools | ⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-24 reviewing the user documentation. `external-tools.md` says auto-detection finds versions from 2020 onwards; `DymolaSettings` loops `while (year > 2020)`, so 2020x itself is never probed. Either the loop should be `>=` or the documentation should say 2021 onwards. Nobody has reported it, which may simply mean nobody uses 2020x any more. **Decided 2026-09-24: the documentation changes** — it now says 2021 onwards. |
| B287 ✅ | **The External Resources “All” chip means “everything else”** | External Resources | ⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-24 reviewing the user documentation. The chip is labelled **All**, with the tooltip “All file types”, but its filter value is `other`: it adds the files no other chip covers, and turning it on alone does not show everything. The documentation's table says “Everything else not covered above”, which matches the behaviour and not the label. Rename the chip (e.g. **Other**) and its tooltip, or make it mean all. **Decided 2026-09-24: it means all.** The chip's value is `all` (`ExternalResources.AllFileTypes`), which passes every file whatever else is selected; the `other` catch-all no longer has a chip of its own and is reached through it. The chip-to-category guards allow exactly that one exception each way, and `MissingChipCountTests` covers the chip on its own and without it. |
| B288 ✅ | **The UI theme saves as soon as it is chosen; every other setting waits for Save Settings** | Settings | ⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-24 reviewing the user documentation. `ui-customization.md` (Saving and Resetting) says changes take effect only after **Save Settings**. Choosing a UI theme saves at once, so the rule has an exception the user cannot see. Probably fine to keep, since a theme is previewed by applying it, but then the documentation should say so and the dialog perhaps too. Decide, then align the other one. **Decided 2026-09-24: intentional** — the change takes effect at once so the user can see the whole application in the scheme and decide whether they like it. `ui-customization.md` now says so, along with the two consequences worth knowing: it saves the tab's UI settings as they stand, and **Reset to Defaults** saves immediately too. |
| B289 ✅ | **A large blank space between the Metrics tab's coverage bars and its trend chart** | Metrics / UI | ⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-24 while testing. MainLayout's inline stylesheet restated MudBlazor's `.mud-grid` rule with `height: 100%` added, from the initial commit, so every MudGrid took the height of whatever contained it. Almost everywhere that height is automatic and 100% of it is nothing; the Metrics page sits in the tab host's flex column (B250), which gives it a definite height, so its coverage grid stretched and pushed the trend down by the difference — about 760px in a 2200px window. Removing the rule outright broke the other thing it was doing: MudExSplitPanel wraps itself in a MudGrid, and the app shell's panes stopped filling the window, which only the documentation screenshots showed. **Fixed** by scoping it to `.mud-grid:has(> .mud-ex-split-panel)`. `MetricsLayoutJourney` asserts the grid ends at its content; it needs a tall window, and passed with the bug in place until it had one. The documentation screenshots come out the same size as before. |
| B290 ✅ | **A class edited from Code Review lost its dependency edges — no classes to go to, and nothing saying why** | Code Review / analysis | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-24 as MSL's `Modelica.Blocks.Continuous.Der` offering no links. It was not the resolver: loading the same 68,877 classes headless, and the user's own split MSL checkout, gave Der its edge to `Interfaces.SISO` every time. The log showed the user had corrected a spelling in Der.mo from Code Review. `ReloadFileAsync` replaces every class in the file with a new node that uses nothing, and the new node does not know who uses it, while the graph goes on saying its dependencies are analysed. The MCP server had refreshed after its edits since it gained them (`GraphRefresh`); none of the desktop app's three Code Review reloads did — the spelling correction, the in-source annotation writer, and the revert before a formatting opt-out. **Fixed** with `ILibraryDataService.RefreshDependenciesAsync`, which re-analyses the reloaded classes and repairs the reverse edges, used by all three and by `GraphRefresh` in place of its own copy. Kept separate from the reload because an edit moving a class between files reloads both, and analysing after the first would resolve the class while it was in neither. `ReloadRefreshPolicyTests` holds every reload in `MLQT.Shared` to it, with the LibraryBrowser revert ledgered (its `VcsFilesChanged` pipeline re-analyses), verified by removing the fix. |
| B291 ✅ | **`EnsureParsed` could return null for a class with source, and dependency analysis then skipped it in silence** | Analysis / threading | ⭐⭐ | S | Found 2026-09-24 investigating B290. It read `ParsedCode` twice - to test it and to return it - and again after parsing, so a tree another reader released in between came back null. `Borrow` releases what it parsed, and the Metrics tab borrows while the deferred analysis runs (the user's log shows both at once), and `AnalyzeDependenciesAsync` takes a null tree as nothing to analyse: the class kept no edges and nothing was reported. Not shown to be what hit Der - B290 was - but the same symptom. **Fixed** by reading the property once and returning the tree it parsed. `ParseTreeBorrowingTests` races a releasing thread against 20,000 calls: 3,728 nulls before, none after. |
| B292 ✅ | **A name imported by an enclosing package resolved to nothing, and dependency analysis ignored plain imports** | Analysis | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Reported 2026-09-24 as MSL's `LimPID` offering links to some of the classes it uses and not others. MSL declares `import Modelica.Units.SI;` once, in `Modelica.Blocks`, and every block below writes `SI.Time`; both resolvers asked only the class writing a name for its imports. Dependency analysis's `ReferenceResolver` was also a drifted copy of `TypeResolver`: it read only aliased imports, matched them by prefix (so `SIx` matched `SI`), and dropped the list form. **Fixed** by making `TypeResolver.ResolveName` the one lookup, which walks outward trying each enclosing class's classes and then its imports, with `ReferenceResolver` delegating to it; each class's imports are read once by `ClassImports.For` and kept on its definition. `EnclosingImportTests` covers the MSL shape through both resolvers and each import form, and fails on the old code. **Effect on the user's MSL check: 21,379 findings to 21,191, all in `MLQT.Style.DeclarationOrder`** - 1,064 gone, 876 new - because a declaration of an `SI` type is now known to be a variable rather than a component; the new ones checked are real (`M_Transformer`'s `SI.Voltage v` after its pins). No other rule moved, and the check takes the same ~6s. |
| B293 ✅ | **Update repository froze the window for minutes after git had finished** | Revision control / threading | ⭐⭐⭐ | M | Reported 2026-09-25 on MSL: the progress bar stayed up and it was not clear whether git had run. It had - the update was a no-op - and the library reload after it finished; then nothing for 2½ minutes, which the first time looked like a hang and was closed. Three stack dumps of the reproduction showed **55 LibGit2Sharp status scans of the one working copy running at once**, one of them on the UI thread, and the process using 6.5 cores; a single scan took 277 ms once they drained. Where they came from: `GetWorkingCopyChanges` cached its answer but every caller that missed the cache started its own query, and a VCS operation makes them all miss together; `RefreshRepositoryAsync` and the Revert loop announced every library and file to the tree separately, and each announcement cost every open tree a status query; `BaselineStatusService` ran its refresh synchronously when a load finished - on the UI thread, since the load is awaited from there - and its throttle limited how often a refresh started, not how many were running (14 at once). Photino made it worse: `InvokeAsync` from a pool thread is a synchronous `SendMessage`, so with the UI thread inside a scan the pool starved and grew by a thread a second - the one-second gaps in the log. Each scan was probably slow because Format All and Revert had rewritten every file (B298). **Fixed**: one query per repository at a time, shared by everyone who asks while it runs, with an invalidation counter so a caller never joins a query older than the change it was told about (`WorkingCopyQuerySharingTests`); the reload and the Revert loop announce once (`SuppressTreeDataChanged`); `IBaselineStatusService.RefreshAsync` queues refreshes on the pool, never two at once and a burst folded into one more run, used by every event and by Code Review on opening (`BaselineStatusQueueTests`). The first version of the sharing waited for the shared query while holding the cache lock its owner needed to publish - its own test hung, and a stack dump of the test run found it. Each test verified by mutation. |
| B294 ✅ | **Update left the file monitor off when it failed, said nothing once git finished, and let a second VCS operation start over it** | Library Browser | ⭐⭐ | S | Found with B293. Update stops the monitor and only the analysis pipeline it hands over to restarts it, so anything that threw before the hand-over left the repository unwatched for the session. The snackbar said "Already up to date" and then the progress bar stayed up through the reload with nothing to account for it. And nothing stopped a second operation: in the reproduction a Revert was still reloading its files one at a time when Update started removing and reloading every library in the same repository. **Fixed** in `LibraryBrowser`: a `try`/`finally` restarts the monitor if the hand-over was not reached and reports the failure; "Reloading libraries…" once the VCS step is done; every VCS entry point runs through `RunVcsOperationAsync`, which refuses a second while one runs, with the buttons disabled meanwhile. `LibraryBrowserUpdateTests`, verified by mutation. The guard covers the browser's own operations, not the analysis pipeline that runs after them - see B296. |
| B295 ✅ | **A git command could hang its operation for good: fetch, push, force push, rebase and Continue Rebase** | Git | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Found by the audit of the other VCS operations made for B293. `RunGitCommand` read stdout to the end before touching stderr, so git writing more to stderr than the pipe holds deadlocked both - `git fetch` lists every updated ref on stderr, so a fetch bringing in a few dozen tags was enough. It also inherited stdin, let git prompt on a terminal nobody could see, had no time limit, and let `rebase --continue` open an editor for the commit message, which with no console is a wait forever. **Fixed**: both streams read at once, stdin closed, `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` (Git Credential Manager's own window still asks), `GIT_EDITOR=true` so Continue keeps the message, and a 15-minute limit after which git and everything it started are stopped and the error says so. `GitCommandRunnerTests` drives each through a git alias; the stream, stdin and timeout tests fail against the old runner. The environment is asserted on the start info, because asked through git those tests passed with the settings deleted - the session running them already had `GIT_EDITOR=true`. |
| B296 ✅ | **The other VCS operations left the file monitor off, reloaded for nothing, or overlapped the analysis pipeline** | Library Browser / dialogs | ⭐⭐ | M | From the audit made for B293. **Monitor left off**: cancelling the Rebase or Merge dialog (Git and SVN) in its conflict phase - by its button, Escape or the backdrop - and a Checkout Revision from VCS History whose reload threw. **Never paused**: Switch Branch, so the checkout reported every file it rewrote. **Reloads with nothing to reload**: Push and Create Branch each removed and reloaded every library. **Overlaps**: the Merge and Rebase dialogs started the analysis pipeline while still open, and the browser then reloaded every library once they closed, so the analysis ran over a graph being rebuilt; the SVN Merge dialog closed itself before its commit, so the browser's reload and the pipeline raced. **Stale after a commit**: committing to an out-of-date SVN working copy updated it with no pause and no reload. A cancelled merge also left the browser showing the libraries from before it, because the result said nothing had happened. **Fixed** with one rule for each half. The monitor: `MonitorPause` stops it and starts it again on disposal unless the restart has been handed to the pipeline, and every operation holds one - the dialogs dispose theirs when they close, which is what catches Escape. The analysis: a dialog records what it did to the working copy in a `VcsDialogOutcome` passed in by the browser - rewritten, and whether a merge or rebase was left with conflicts - and `LibraryBrowser.AfterVcsDialogAsync` reloads and only then starts the pipeline, and not at all over unresolved conflicts, since the formatter does not skip a file with conflict markers in it. No dialog starts the pipeline itself any more; the SVN Merge dialog commits before it closes. Push and Create Branch reload nothing. The dialogs' busy flags are reset in `finally`. `MonitorPauseTests`, `VcsDialogMonitorTests` (a rebase and a merge cancelled in their conflicts, and an abort as the control) and the `AfterVcsDialogAsync` cases in `LibraryBrowserUpdateTests`, each verified by mutation. What was found and left is B301. |
| B297 ✅ | **SVN commands had no time limit** | SVN | ⭐⭐ | S | From the audit made for B293. `SvnCli` already read both streams at once and passed `--non-interactive`, so it could not deadlock on a pipe or prompt, but a server that accepted the connection and then stalled blocked update, commit, switch or merge - and the dialog waiting on it - for good; and stdin was inherited whenever nothing was piped in. **Fixed** in `SvnCli.Execute`, which `Run` and `RunForBytes` now share: stdin is always redirected and closed, and **a command that writes nothing for 10 minutes** is stopped with everything it started and reported as a failure that says so. Silence rather than running time, unlike git's 15-minute limit (B295): a checkout or update of a large repository runs as long as it runs, but svn reports each file as it goes, so a command still working is one still writing. `SvnCommandRunnerTests` drives `Execute` with git running shell aliases, since it takes the executable - a silent command is stopped, a chatty one running three times the limit is not, stdin reaches its end, piped text still arrives, and output past the pipe buffer on both streams does not deadlock; the silence and stdin tests fail against the old behaviour. The 704-test `RevisionControl` suite, SVN integration included, passes unchanged. **Follow-up, found by the whole-solution run during B300**: decoding svn's output through a `StreamReader` tripped `ModelicaFileAccessPolicyTests`, which that suite alone does not run; it now decodes the bytes directly. |
| B298 ✅ | **After a bulk rewrite, every LibGit2Sharp status query re-read and hashed the whole working copy** | Git / performance | ⭐⭐ | S | A theory from B293, **measured 2026-09-25** on a clone of MSL (10,231 files) with every file's timestamp moved and its content unchanged, which is what Format All and a revert leave: status took 200-250 ms with a fresh index, then 38.9 s for the first query after the touch (cold file cache) and **7.1-7.3 s for every query after it**, and LibGit2Sharp never wrote the index back. Eight at once took 22 s each - the shape of B293's 55. `git update-index --refresh` took 0.9 s, after which a status took 232 ms. **Fixed** in `GitRevisionControlSystem.GetWorkingCopyChanges`: a status slower than 2 seconds is followed by `git update-index -q --refresh`, which records the new timestamps and stages nothing, and the log says so. Self-healing rather than tied to Format All, so it covers a revert, a branch switch in another tool, or anything else that moves timestamps. On the same clone through MLQT's own call: 8.3 s for the first query after the touch, then 267 ms and 246 ms. `GitIndexRefreshTests`: a slow status rewrites the index, an ordinary one does not, and a file that really changed is still reported and not staged; the first fails with the refresh removed. The failed read seen in the reproduction (`could not read (expected 1431710 bytes, read 1431647)`, a status racing Format All's writes) is left as it was: logged, harmless, and the next status is correct. |
| B299 ✅ | **The Metrics tab did its work on whatever thread announced a change** | Metrics / threading | ⭐ | S | From the audit made for B293. `MetricsDashboard.OnLibrariesChanged` cleared the component's figures, read every history file and rebuilt the trend on the thread that raised it - a library load's pool thread, or the window's when a reload is awaited from the UI - while a render could be reading the same fields. Doing it found two more of the same shape in the same file: `OnStyleCheckProgress` and `OnDeferredAnalysisCompleted` recounted findings on the checker's thread and marshalled only the render. **Fixed**: the history is read on the pool and applied on the dispatcher, numbered so a read that finishes after a newer one is dropped (a reload raises the event once per library); the two recounts run inside `InvokeAsync`, as `OnFindingsChanged` already did. **Worth knowing for the next one**: `InvokeAsync` moves work onto the dispatcher but does not free whoever raised the event - it runs inline for a caller when the dispatcher is idle, and on the desktop host it is a synchronous `SendMessage` - so work that is slow as well as thread-unsafe needs `Task.Run` first, as the history read has. `MetricsDashboardEventThreadTests`: announcing a library change does not wait for the history read, and both recounts happen on the dispatcher; all three fail against the old handlers. |
| B300 ✅ | **After Format All Files, the top-level packages showed the default folder icon instead of their own** | Library Browser / icons | ⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-25 on MSL while testing B293, and again after a first fix that did not work. **The cause**: the library browser draws `IconSvg` straight off the class objects it holds, and the `ModelDefinition.ModelicaCode` setter blanked it along with everything else derived from the code (B258). Format All sets every class's code and does not refresh the tree, so the next render drew an empty icon - the folder - for every package on screen, until something rebuilt the tree; a revert does, which is why the icons came back after one. **Found by running Format All headlessly** over a copy of MSL: every top-level icon was present after a re-render, so the service was right and what the tree held in between was not. **Fixed**: the setter now marks the icon to be rendered again (`IconRendered = false`) and leaves the SVG, so the old icon is shown until the tree next asks - for a reformatted class the right one, since formatting moves text, not graphics. `IconCacheInvalidationTests` now asserts that, and fails with the blanking put back; `IconAfterCodeChangeTests` holds the other half - the next refresh draws the new icon, and none at all for a class whose icon was taken out, since nothing blanks it in advance any more. **The first fix is not in**: it assumed the icon was rendered while `Modelica.Icons.Package` was out of the graph mid-reload, and added a renderer callback and a graph version counter to retry such a miss. That was never observed, the headless run showed no miss, and B293's one announcement per reload already keeps the tree from rendering mid-reload, so it was withdrawn rather than kept untested. |
| B301 ✅ | **A repository sharing its working copy with another was not fully covered by a VCS operation on either** | Revision control | ⭐ | S | Found doing B296. Two libraries checked out in one Git or SVN tree are two repositories, and every VCS operation acts on the tree, but what followed one reached only the repository whose button was pressed. **Switch Branch** reloaded every repository on the working copy yet analysed only that one. **Update, Merge, Rebase and Checkout Revision** reloaded only that one, and Update refreshed only its revision info. **The file monitor** keeps one watcher per folder with a subscriber per repository, so pausing one repository left the watcher running for the other and the operation's writes were reported to it. **Fixed**: `IRepositoryService.GetRepositoriesSharingWorkingCopy` names the group; `MonitorPause` holds off every repository in it that was being watched, and starts only those again, so a reference-only repository is never started by a pause; `LibraryBrowser` reloads every one and then analyses every one, as do Switch Branch and VCS History; Update refreshes the whole group's revision info. The analysis pipeline, now started once per repository, runs **one at a time** behind a gate in `MainLayout`, so two do not format and re-analyse over the same graph at once - that gate has no test of its own. `SharedWorkingCopyTests` (two libraries in one Git checkout and one in another, against real repositories), the group cases in `MonitorPauseTests` and `LibraryBrowserUpdateTests`, each verified by mutation. |
| B269 ✅ | **A large file in diff mode took the whole application down** | Code Review | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-21: opening a large FMU model with the Code Review tab in diff mode left the red *"An unhandled error has occurred. Reload"* banner, and the page then reported the file was too large to display. Caught in the debugger as `leftEl.addEventListener is not a function` out of `diffViewer.js:initSyncScroll`, thrown back through `DiffViewer.OnAfterRenderAsync`. **A file too big for the O(m·n) diff renders a message instead of the two panes**, so `@ref` never runs and both element references stay default — but `OnAfterRenderAsync` asked only what the *view mode* was, not what had been rendered, and called the interop anyway. A default `ElementReference` still serialises to an object, which is truthy, so it also went straight past `if (!leftEl \|\| !rightEl) return;` in the script. **Three fixes, and the third is the one that matters**: `ShowsPanes` is now one property the markup's last branch and the interop call both ask; the script checks for `addEventListener` rather than for truthiness; and the call is wrapped, because scroll synchronisation failing is worth a line in the log and is never worth the window. The same crash was reachable through "No content to display", which is not a large file at all. |
| B270 ✅ | **Reloading after an error asked for a project while one was still open** | Startup | ⭐⭐⭐ | S | Found with B269, because B269 is what put the Reload button on screen. Blazor's error banner offers Reload for any unhandled exception, and in a webview host that rebuilds the component tree while the services stay where they are — they are singletons registered by `AddMlqtCore` in the host's `Program.cs`. `RunStartUpAsync` therefore ran a second time against a process that already had a project open: the user was shown **Select Project** with the previous project's packages still visible in the library browser behind the dialog, and answering it would have loaded a second copy of everything into one graph. `MainLayout.StartupAlreadyRan` now asks the surviving service how many repositories are loaded — deliberately not a flag on the component, since the component is the thing that was just replaced — and skips the sequence, re-syncing only the project name. Zero loaded repositories is a genuine first run *or* a reload of a session with nothing open, and re-running startup is right for both. |
| B257 ✅ | **The combined analysis step reports itself as "Analysing dependencies" for the whole of it** | Performance / UI | ⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-20 as *"analyse dependencies took a lot longer although the style checking then seemed to be pretty quick"* - and measured, that is exactly what happens and nothing is wrong with it. The deferred steps can run **combined**, and in that path style checking is interleaved with dependency analysis rather than following it. End to end on the same library the same afternoon: separate path deps 81.1s and all style checking complete at **353.4s**; combined path deps 244.3s and complete at **321.1s**. The combined path is **32 seconds faster** and its dependency phase looks three times slower, because it is carrying the other half. **The label is the only thing wrong.** A user watching a four-minute "Analysing dependencies" has no way to know style checking is most of it, which is how a faster path comes to be reported as a regression - it was, here, until the like-for-like comparison was found (combined runs on 2026-08-17 took 289.0s and 274.3s at the same scale, so today was the fastest of the three). Name the step for what it is doing, or report the two counts separately within it. **WP13**, with B261: WP4 filed this from its own measurements, named it in its prose, listed it nowhere and shipped without it. **Done 2026-09-24 (WP13).** Found in the log, where a performance question in this repository is always answered: the combined pass logged its start as `Running deferred dependency analysis + style checking (combined)`, its end as `Running deferred dependency analysis`, and then a `Running deferred style checking (combined with dependency analysis)` completion with **no start at all** — so matched by name, dependencies took the whole pass and style checking took none, which is the 244.3s the report read. Its closing message said only *Dependency analysis complete.* `DeferredDependencyStep` now names the pass once, for both ends of the log and both messages, and it is decided from whether style checking is really carried (asked for **and** not already run) rather than from whether it was asked for, which was a second way the log could describe a pass that did not happen. The startup dialog already spun both rows for the combined pass and was left alone. |
| B258 ✅ | **Startup still stutters, and reading the code has found only things already fixed** | Performance | ⭐⭐ | S | Reported 2026-09-20 after B190: the window is responsive during dependency analysis and style checking now, but still stutters during the initial load, and the user connects it to the library browser being populated. **Three candidates read and all three were already handled**, which is why this is an item rather than a fix: the bulk load already wraps itself in `SuppressTreeDataChanged()` so 122 library loads announce **once**; `CheckForUncommittedChangesAsync` already does its working-copy query inside `Task.Run`; and `RefreshTreeItems` builds only the **top level**, not 39,860 nodes. **One thing found and not yet priced**: `RefreshDescendantParserErrors` walks `GetAllModels()` - 69,141 of them - on the dispatcher, and the answer is global while the field is per component, so N repositories compute the same set N times. Whether that is the stutter is unmeasured. `RefreshTreeItems` is now timed per step and logs when a refresh costs 50ms or more, which is how B253 was found after two wrong guesses - **run it once and let the log name the step**. Also unexamined: `OnLibrariesChanged` is *not* suppressed during the bulk load, so it fires 122 times, and `MetricsDashboard`'s handler does a `ReportableModels()` walk and a `LoadAllHistory()` disk read synchronously on the loader thread each time - cheap for the loader if that page is not mounted, unknown if it is. **✅ The log named it on the first run.** `Tree refresh for 'Claytex' took 1522ms (top level **1477ms**, annotate 0ms, parser errors 44ms, expansion 0ms)` - and many like it. 97% of a tree refresh is `GetTopLevelModelsAsync`, and inside that `PrepareModelForDisplay` **renders every top-level class's icon**: resolving each one's base classes and parsing them to do it, on the dispatcher, on every refresh. Nothing cached it because it could not safely be cached - `IconSvg` lived on `ModelNode`, outside the invalidation that `ModelicaCode`'s setter already does for `ParsedCode`, `Coverage` and `Suppressions`. **So it moved to where its siblings live**: rendered once per class, discarded with the rest when the code changes, with `IconRendered` separate from the SVG being null because most classes have no icon and “asked, and there is none” has to be as cheap to remember as an answer. `ModelNode.IconSvg` stays as a facade. Four tests, the three that matter being about **invalidation rather than speed** - a cache that is slow is a disappointment, one that keeps an answer past the question is a wrong icon on screen - verified by deleting the invalidation and watching three of four fail. **The parser-errors step is 44ms and was left**: it walks all 69,141 models for an answer that is global while the field is per component, so N repositories compute it N times, but at 3% of the refresh it is not what was reported. **Second round, on the same evidence.** With icons cached, the log showed the walk that had been 3% become the whole of a refresh: `Tree refresh for 'Library' took 873ms (top level 0ms, annotate 0ms, **parser errors 872ms**, expansion 0ms)`. `GetAllModels()` builds a 69,141-entry hash set and filters every node in the graph, **under the service's lock** - which is why the outlier lands on whichever tree asks while a load still holds it, and why the ones after it are 12-65ms. The answer is a property of the project and every repository's browser computed it separately, so it moved to `ILibraryDataService.ModelsWithDescendantParserErrors()`: worked out once, handed to all of them, dropped in `RaiseTreeDataChanged` - **whether or not the announcement is suppressed**, because a bulk load is exactly when it goes stale and the single announcement at the end must not be served a set built before the libraries arrived. Four tests, the load-bearing one being that loading another library rebuilds it, verified by removing the invalidation. **A mock had to be taught the new method**: Moq returns null for a reference type, and the fixture now says what the interface promises rather than the component defending against it. **Third round: the work moved off the dispatcher.** With both caches in, the first refresh after a load still spent about a second in `top level` - the icons genuinely being rendered, once, but on the thread the desktop host pumps window messages on. `GetTopLevelModelsAsync` and `GetChildModelsAsync` both **returned a completed task**, so awaiting them ran everything on the caller: the name had been promising something it did not do. Both now run on the pool, and both **prepare outside the lock** - deciding which library owns a class needs it, rendering that class's icon does not, and holding it through work that reaches into the graph and parses other classes is what made an unrelated `GetAllModels` wait 872ms. **One consequence handled**: two trees can now prepare the same class at once, so the rendered flag is set *after* the render rather than before - claiming it first would let the second caller see “already rendered” with no SVG and silently drop the icon. **That ordering is reasoned, not pinned**: a test was written for it, the inverted ordering passed it every time, and the window between the two writes is too narrow to hit on demand. What the test does hold is that eight simultaneous callers all get a usable answer, and the test says so rather than claiming more. **Fourth round, and the timestamps gave it away.** Four trees reporting 1,421ms, 1,423ms, 1,429ms and 1,420ms and **finishing on the same millisecond** are not four pieces of work - they are four concurrent passes over the same set. Every tree asks `GetTopLevelModelsAsync` for *all* top-level classes and filters to its own repository afterwards, so a project with four repositories prepared the same hundred-odd libraries four times, and because they ran together none of them found the icons the others were rendering. **Setting the rendered flag after the render made that worse**, which is the honest cost of that correctness fix: before it, three of the four skipped. Now one preparer runs at a time - the first pass does the work and the rest find it done, the same wall clock for a quarter of the effort. **And the instrumentation had become misleading**: `top level` is no longer time on the dispatcher, so a large number there stopped meaning a blocked window. The log line now separates what is measured on the UI thread from what is awaited off it, because a measurement that outlives the thing it was measuring is worse than none. |

---

## End-of-branch review of `backlog` — 2026-09-25

Opened 2026-09-25 by a review of the whole `backlog` branch (164 commits, B170–B301) against
`main`. Eight reviewers read the code in parallel, one per area, each told to find what the
branch introduced or left unfinished and to quote the lines behind every claim; the most severe of
each area's findings were re-checked against the code before being written here. Nothing was built
